Roll-up-equals-sum asserted in `turnover.test.ts` | PRD §9 | M | R-044, R-030 | MVP |
| 76 | ✅ R-076 | The five weekly saved views: rent roll + delinquency aging (existing `/money/rent-roll`) · open work orders by age and priority (`/workorders`, now sorted) · vacancy and turn status (`/vacancies`, extended) · cash summary per entity **(no reserve target - unconfigured anywhere, R-082 owns it)** · upcoming critical dates next 60 days (D-66) | PRD RPT-04 | M | R-075, R-050 | MVP |
| 77 | ✅ R-077 | **Compliance calendar**: rental licenses and registrations, CO and periodic city inspections, smoke/CO certifications, lead disclosures for pre-1978, HOA obligations, property tax due dates **and assessment-appeal windows**, Section 8 inspection and recertification dates, entity-level items (LLC annual reports, registered agent) via a generic `ComplianceItem`/`ComplianceCompletion` (insurance renewals stay on the existing `InsurancePolicy`, unioned in rather than duplicated); lead-time alerts + overdue escalation (`alert-job.ts`) and a permanent completion log answering "when was this last done" in one lookup; closes the honest gap R-050/R-076 both named by wiring into `/reports/dates` (D-67) | PRD PROP-05 | M | R-008, R-011, R-016 | MVP |
| 78 | ✅ R-078 | **The basis is a parameter, and that is what the row did not say.** OQ-8 was answered "both" (D-71) — the right call for a reason the question surfaced: the SAME work order books in two different tax YEARS depending on it (closed 28 December, invoice paid 6 January), so a basis baked in as a default is a silent decision about which return a deduction lands on. Cash reads `LedgerEntry` rows that **carry a payment**, accrual reads `Charge` by `dueOn`. The cash rule is `paymentId != null` rather than a `LedgerEntryType` filter: `webhook.ts` sets it on exactly the rows where money moved, including the **unlinked subscription remainder — the largest income line in the system**, which a three-value type filter would have silently missed. `CapitalImprovement` is a standalone record with an optional `@unique` work-order link, not a flag on `WorkOrder`, because PROP-07 names improvements that predate the platform and have no job to flag; the link is **load-bearing**, since the export excludes exactly those jobs from deductible repairs and two rows on one job would let the same dollars be claimed twice — once on line 14, again as a depreciable asset. **Nine Schedule E lines cannot be filled at all** (advertising, auto/travel, commissions, insurance, management fees, mortgage interest, other interest, supplies, taxes) and are **named on the page with the reason** rather than left blank, because a missing expense line reads as a zero and a zero overstates income. `DEPOSIT` is held off income as a liability and `OTHER` is refused outright — mapping an unclassified charge to rents received is a guess wearing the costume of a fact. **`mapped + excepted + outOfYear + capitalised === facts` is asserted on both bases and proven to fail by removing one counter**; a count that does not have to add up cannot catch a dropped row, which is the whole of "nothing silently dropped". **Found along the way:** the year boundary is R-042's bug class pointed at a tax year — `invoicePaidAt` is a real timestamp, so it reads through the PROPERTY's zone, never the server's (UTC on Vercel), or a Texas payment on New Year's Eve lands in the wrong return. **Left behind:** `UtilityBill` records no payment date, so the owner's absorbed share is a cash-basis exception that names its own fix (D-71); no per-entity chart-of-accounts overrides — OQ-8's other half is still open; depreciation deliberately not computed | PRD RPT-03, PROP-07; D-42, D-71 | M | R-035, R-030, R-015 | MVP |
| 79 | ✅ R-079 | Vendor management: new `/vendors` CRUD surface (none existed - records were database-only before this), trades/service areas/license, **W-9 gate** surfaced as a picker warning rather than a silent filter, **COI expiry tracking** flagged the same way, preferred-and-fallback ranking (`fallbackVendorsForTrade()`, built in R-025 but never actually called until now), cumulative calendar-year payment totals by method for 1099-NEC (`jobCostCents()`), and **invoice status visible to the vendor** (not_received → approved → paid, tolerance-based auto-routing, derived from existing approval logic, not a stored status) (D-68) | PRD MAINT-11, MAINT-09 | M | R-025, R-016 | Phase 2 |
| 80 | ✅ R-080 | Preventive maintenance + **seasonal batch templates**: a free-form, PM-created `PreventiveMaintenanceTemplate` (name, trade, cadence in months) covers filters/HVAC/gutters/winterization/smoke-CO/water-heater/dryer-vent/sump-pump/pest/well-septic/chimney — nothing hardcoded, a PM adds what their portfolio needs. New `/maintenance/preventive` page: due count read live off the last CLOSED job tagged `WorkOrder.pmTemplateId` (D-69), **one click creates the batch across every unit in scope**, auto-assigned by a new `vendorCoversProperty()` territory match composed with the existing `fallbackVendorsForTrade()` ranking — unmatched units stay unassigned for manual pickup. **Smoke/CO "logged at every entry" named as a gap**: no generic entry-log exists in this product; it's just another recurring template, not per-visit-hooked | PRD MAINT-08 | M | R-024, R-073 | Phase 2 |
| 81 | ✅ R-081 | **SPLIT INTO THREE (the L-splitting rule below, applied at the start rather than discovered halfway).** The three halves answer different questions for different people and share almost no query surface: an operating report a PM reads weekly, a packet a CPA reads once a year, and a funnel a leasing agent reads daily. Building them as one item would have meant one enormous session and a single commit nobody could review | PRD RPT-05, RPT-06, RPT-07, MAINT-10 | L | R-078, R-079, R-075 | Phase 2 |
| 81a | ✅ R-081a | **The operating reports** (RPT-05): per-property operating snapshot — income, maintenance spend, vacancy days, ticket counts, the "which house is a lemon" view — plus monthly P&L per property and entity, vendor spend by **trade** (not by vendor; `vendorPaymentTotalsForYear` already answers per-vendor), **renewal rate** and turnover cost. **RPT-09 makes "P&L" bounded**: full financial statements are an explicit Won't — "QuickBooks' job" — so this is an OPERATING P&L off the same sources R-078 already maps, never a general ledger. **Built by RE-GROUPING R-078's export lines, not re-fetching** (D-72): `taxExportFacts` is the only place that knows the ledger sign-flip, the capitalised-job exclusion and read-the-timestamp-in-the-property's-zone, and all three are silent when wrong, so a monthly P&L is that same list grouped by `(propertyId, month)`. Spend-by-trade is derived from those same lines with the trade lookup keyed on ids already in the report, so the trade columns cannot disagree with the expense total above them. **Trade is what the work WAS, never `Vendor.trades`** — that column is a list, and a two-trade vendor would either double-count or be picked from arbitrarily. **Defaults to accrual where the tax export defaults to cash**, because on cash R-078 sends owner-absorbed utility bills to the exception list and a cash operating P&L understates expenses by exactly that, silently. **Found along the way:** a real a11y defect the gate caught and review would not — the `overflow-x-auto` table wrappers were not focusable, so a keyboard user could not scroll them, and axe flagged it **only on mobile-chrome** while desktop passed the same page in the same run. **Left behind:** no CSV (RPT-05 does not ask, unlike RPT-02/03); quarterly grouping not built; the P&L inherits R-078's nine unfillable Schedule E lines, so "net" is net of what this product records | PRD RPT-05; RPT-09 (bound); D-71, D-72 | M | R-078, R-079, R-075 | Phase 2 |
| 81b | ✅ R-081b | **The year-end tax packet, as a screen and one CSV** (RPT-07): Schedule E **per property** (R-078's lines are per entity and already carry `propertyId`, so this is a grouping, not new money math), CapEx/fixed-asset schedule, security-deposit **liability** — a BALANCE of what is held, not deposits received in the period, which is the export's existing `DEPOSIT_LIABILITY` section and a different question — **1099-NEC candidate list** across vendors with W-9 status, built on R-079's `FORM_1099_THRESHOLD_CENTS`/`requiresForm1099`, and **mortgage interest from a recorded 1098**. **Owner decision 2026-08-21:** the archived-PDF form of the packet is split to R-081d and this row ships the screen + CSV first; and the 1098 is RECORDED rather than reconciled — a new `MortgageAnnualStatement` (per mortgage, per tax year: interest, principal, escrow, the form as a `Document`) gives Schedule E line 12 a real number, labelled as entered from the 1098 rather than derived from payments this product never tracked. **Mileage stays unbuilt** — RPT-07 says "if captured" and nothing captures it. **The correctness point of the whole item turned out to be the 1099-NEC figure**: a CARD payment is reported by the PROCESSOR on a 1099-K, not by the payer on a 1099-NEC, so $400 by card plus $300 by cheque is a $300 1099-NEC and a vendor can sit UNDER the threshold despite $700 paid — which is what RPT-07 means by "by payment method". Deposit liability respects the disposition date for the same class of reason: a deposit disposed of in March 2027 was still held in full on 31 December 2026, and a naive sum gets that wrong while looking right. **Found along the way:** `Deposit.heldCents`'s own schema comment said it "is what is still held", which reads as already net and would double-count a disposition — it is gross and never decremented, as `rent-roll.ts` and `computeDisposition` both already assumed. **Left behind:** no document upload for the 1098 (`documentId` exists, nothing sets it); no TIN/EIN on `Vendor`; seven Schedule E lines still unfillable, down from nine | PRD RPT-07; D-71, D-73 | M | R-078, R-079 | Phase 2 |
| 81d | ✅ R-081d | **The tax packet as an archived artifact** — split out of R-081b by owner decision (2026-08-21) so the numbers landed first. One PDF of every schedule, persisted as a `Document` with an audit row, following R-083's attorney packet rather than R-078's CSV: **a packet is a claim about the record on a date, and the record keeps moving**, so each export is separately archived (`generateStorageKey`, never a deterministic key the next export would overwrite). **The item turned out to be about where the packet could live at all** (D-74): `Document` was property-keyed and a year-end packet is per ENTITY — Schedule E splits by address but deposit liability and the 1099-NEC list are entity-wide — so attaching it to an arbitrary house would be a false claim and leaving both keys null would make it **unreachable**, since the file route scopes staff access through the owning record and correctly refuses a document with nothing to check against. New `Document.legalEntityId` (the pair `ComplianceItem` has carried since R-077), a file-route entity branch, and the same one-line fix for entity-level compliance completions, whose documents have been undownloadable since R-077. **Only the Form 1098s are attached**, and the rule is worth keeping: every other schedule is derived from records this system can reproduce, while a 1098 is a figure transcribed off a form this product never saw. D-50's index governs — a 1098 with no uploaded copy, or one that will not parse, is named in the document, on the index and on the audit row, which also makes R-081b's deliberate upload gap visible on the artifact. **Found along the way:** `ATTORNEY_PACKET` has been written since R-083 and was never in the closed `DOCUMENT_TYPES`, so `retentionCutoff` threw on any portfolio holding one — latent only because `documentsPastRetention` has no caller yet. Fixed in the vocabulary AND in the lookup, which no longer trusts a cast across the database boundary. The exhibit index moved to `packages/core/documents/exhibits.ts`; two copies of an honesty rule is one copy that stops being honest. **Left behind:** no CapEx invoices in the bundle (named as available from the property file, not silently absent); still nothing uploads the 1098 itself | PRD RPT-07; D-50, D-74 | S | R-081b | Phase 2 |
| 81c | ✅ R-081c | **Leasing funnel and maintenance analytics** (RPT-06, MAINT-10): two new pages, `/reports/leasing` and `/reports/maintenance`. **The correctness of the whole item is that a funnel counts PEOPLE, not events** (D-75) — a prospect who rebooked twice has three `Showing` rows and is one person who viewed the home, so dividing rows by rows reports 300% conversion for the ordinary act of moving an appointment. Conversion is measured **within the cohort that reached the previous stage**, which cannot exceed 100%, and prospects who reached a stage without the one before it are reported as `skipped` rather than inflating a ratio they were never in — applying without a booked viewing is ordinary, not an error. The window is the **inquiry date for every stage**, or each conversion would be a ratio of two different populations. `ListingLead` visits sit beside the funnel and are **never divided into it**: a visit has no person attached and nothing keys it to an inquiry. `APPROVED_WITH_CONDITIONS` counts as approved. Days-to-fill is a **median of the vacancies that ended** — a mean follows one roof replacement, and a still-vacant unit's count has not finished happening. Maintenance half lands **four formulas that existed with no caller**: `resolutionByPriority` and `costPerUnitPerMonth` (R-075 named R-076/R-081a as their first callers and neither needed them) and `WorkOrder.reopenedAt`/`reopenCount`, written since R-030 and read by nothing. New: `repeatIssues` (chains rather than resetting at 90 days — four leaks eighty days apart are one chronic problem, and cutting the chain hides exactly the pattern), `reopenRate` (denominator is jobs that CLOSED, or the rate improves by having a backlog) and `vendorCosts`. Merged tickets are excluded, and here that is load-bearing: two people reporting one leak would themselves be a repeat. A vendor row's two halves are **attributed differently on purpose** — cost to whoever invoiced, reopen rate to the vendor captured when the tenant answered (MAINT-07). **Found along the way:** a null conversion has two causes that must not share a label — the first row has no previous stage, a later one is null because nobody reached the stage before it, and one label for both told the reader that "Approved" is where the funnel starts. **Left behind:** no cost-per-channel (no ad-spend record exists anywhere; named on the page rather than shown as a zero); no `signed` funnel stage; no CSV | PRD RPT-06, MAINT-10; D-75 | M | R-081a, R-064 | Phase 2 |
| 82 | ✅ R-082 | **The split category IS the Schedule E key, and that is what made PAY-10 small.** The row reads as two features and the export turned out to be one line of work: R-081a's operating P&L does not re-fetch anything, it groups the SAME `ExportLine`s `buildTaxExport` produces — so one new fact kind in `packages/core/tax/export.ts` reached Schedule E, the per-property P&L and the tax packet at once, with no second income/expense pipeline to keep in step. A `VendorInvoice` with `VendorInvoiceSplit` rows (property + category + amount + optional work order), splits required to sum to the vendor's printed total **exactly, no tolerance** — the amounts are typed off paper, so an off-by-one is a typo and the moment to catch it is while the paper is in hand. **The double-count guard is the correctness of the whole half** (D-77): a work order named by a split is excluded from repairs exactly as a capitalised one is, enforced three ways because a double deduction is silent in every screenshot — a UNIQUE `workOrderId`, a write that refuses a job already carrying a `CapitalImprovement`, and `splitInvoiced` as its own counted exit door in the reconciliation identity rather than folded into `capitalised`. Side effect worth knowing: seven `UNSOURCED_LINES` entries stopped being true, since advertising, insurance, supplies, commissions, management fees, travel and property tax are now all recordable — reworded from "cannot fill" to "nothing on them", and any line with money on it drops off. **PAY-11's reserve stores nothing it cannot honestly know** (D-76): the target and the balance are both typed in with a required as-of date (there is no bank feed — distributions, mortgage principal and purchase capital are all invisible, so a balance derived from operating cash would overstate it quietly), and the entity figure is the sum of its properties, never a second stored number. The crude capital plan adds **no schema at all** — ages come from `CapitalImprovement.inServiceOn`, `Appliance.installedOn` or `Property.yearBuilt` as an explicit assumption, never a guess, and an undated component accrues zero rather than inventing precision; recording the replacement is what both resets the clock and swaps the national default for what this owner actually paid on this house | PRD PAY-10, PAY-11 | M | R-078, R-015 | Phase 2 |

## Milestone 8: Phase 2 — the risk workflows

Twenty years of scar tissue, encoded. Each is a case file with timestamps and deadline clocks; none of them make a legal judgment on the owner's behalf.

| # | ID | Item | PRD/Feature | Size | Depends on | Phase |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 83 | ✅ R-083 | **Eviction case files.** Pay-or-quit generation and service logging needed NO new machinery — R-051 already built it, including the three-valued `permittedByJurisdiction` verdict stored at the moment of service. What was missing was the consequence: `cureClock()` derives the cure period from the EARLIEST GOOD service, and **`readyToFile()` refuses a filing that would be premature or founded on service the state does not name** — the two mistakes that get a case dismissed and everything restarted. An unconfigured cure period never blocks filing and never guesses a deadline. `EvictionStage` enum (order enforced in core, closes from anywhere because settling always beats a writ), `EvictionCost` (first owner-side outlay in the schema; lost rent deliberately derived, not entered), cash-for-keys as a first-class outcome, and a one-click **attorney packet** assembling case summary + statement + every notice with its proof + lease + photos, naming on its index anything it could not attach (D-50). New `eviction.manage` permission; open/close both REASON_REQUIRED. **Never files anything anywhere** (D-70) | PRD PAY-14 | L | R-047, R-052, R-051 | Phase 2 |
| 84 | ✅ R-084 | **The row said "effects declared per type as configuration" and that one clause is the whole item.** The shape carried from the storage build was a boolean per effect, ticked by hand when the hold is placed — which makes "does a bankruptcy hold stop late fees" a question about what one person remembered on one Tuesday, and 11 U.S.C. §362 does not care what was ticked (D-78). So `HOLD_DEFINITIONS` in `packages/core/holds` is the only policy, the migration carries **no effect columns at all**, and **no guard anywhere names a hold type** — every one asks `leasesHalted(ids, effect)`, so a seventh type is covered by all four guards on the day it is added. Four guards, each one query per property rather than per lease: late-fee assessment (both passes), `pastGraceLeaseIds` (which is the server-side re-check R-044 already calls "immediately before anything is sent", so a hold placed while the screen was open stops the chase the same way a payment does), the daily rent-due notices, and announcement audiences. **`do_not_contact` deliberately does NOT halt late fees** (D-80) — asking not to be contacted is not asking to be forgiven — and that asymmetry has its own named test so a tidy-up cannot flatten it. **The banner warns and blocks nothing** on notice and eviction screens (D-79): serving is sometimes lawful under all six types, and blocking would make the operator's workaround *lifting the hold*, which loses the record. The one hard refusal is access-code issuance. "Manager-or-above to lift SCRA" is a second MFA-gated permission, not a role hierarchy that D-5 forbids (D-81). Lifted holds keep their row — "was the stay in force the day that fee was assessed" needs a row that survived being lifted. **Found along the way:** nothing carried the tenant record the row names — there is no `/tenants` route, so the lease page is it; and a real bug latent since R-072 — `e2e/leases.spec.ts`'s teardown could not delete a lease its own test had ENDED, because the auto-opened `TurnoverProject` holds it with a `Restrict` | PRD RISK-12, RISK-11; D-78, D-79, D-80, D-81 | M | R-083, R-040 | Phase 2 |
| 85 | ✅ R-085 | **The one statute in this product that is NOT jurisdiction configuration** (D-82). D-4 is categorical about statutory numbers living in versioned config; §§3931/3955 are federal, uniform across fifty states and **preempt** state law, so a `JurisdictionRule` column would be one correct value in fifty rows and fifty chances to get one wrong. The consequence is not cosmetic: an SCRA termination deliberately skips `noticePeriodCheck`, because warning a servicemember that their federal right is "short notice" and demanding an override reason for it is the product getting the law backwards. **The "military-status flag" the row asks for is R-084's `military_scra` hold — no second flag exists anywhere**, and an active-duty certificate now places that hold **automatically** (D-86), the only automatic hold in the product: every other type rests on a human judgement, this one on a signed federal certificate just read into the record. §3955's date is **computed, never typed** — 30 days after the next rent due **strictly after** delivery, which is a whole month away from "on or after" and is the first unit test. **The judgment gate is the exception D-79 predicts rather than contradicts** (D-85): a default judgment with no DMDC search on file is REFUSED, because unlike serving under a hold it is not sometimes-lawful — it is voidable on the servicemember's application — and the workaround is to go and run the search rather than to destroy a record. A contested judgment is not gated at all, so "did the tenant appear" is radios, not a checkbox. **`INDETERMINATE` is first-class and also refuses** (D-84): a DMDC no-match is not a negative, and `affidavitLookupFor` takes the **worst** result across each tenant's latest search so a second adult's clean certificate cannot mask the first's active duty. **No adapter** — DMDC has no per-tenant API to simulate, so the product records that a human ran the search and stores the certificate (D-83, D-15's call applied) **Found along the way:** a duplicate accessible name I introduced (two "Forwarding address" inputs on one lease page), and a **latent race in `leases.spec.ts`** — `getByText('active')` is a substring match that was already true before the click, so the database read raced the mutation; it won for a year and started losing the moment a panel saying "on active duty" landed above it, and the obvious fix (wait for the button to vanish) is the same bug again because `SubmitButton` renames itself to "Working…" while pending — every UI signal there resolves before the write lands, so the fix is to poll the fact | PRD RISK-12; D-82, D-83, D-84, D-85, D-86 | S→M | R-084 | Phase 2 |
| 86 | ✅ R-086 | **"Distinct from a pet" is enforced at the MONEY, not by a type column** (D-87). This product has never had a pet entity — pets exist only as `Listing.petsAllowed`, a `WorkOrder.petWarning` flag and two `ChargeType` values — so inventing an `Animal` table to be distinct *from* would be building an unrequested feature to make a sentence look tidy. An approved request IS the assistance-animal record, and `PET_MONEY_TYPES`/`petMoneyAllowed` in core are what stop the charge. **The rule names all three pet-money types including the two nothing writes yet** (D-88): only `PET_RENT` has a live writer, and guarding just that caller would leave `PET_FEE` and the `PET` deposit for somebody who never read the rule — which is how the second violation happens. **Requesting documentation does not pause the clock** (D-89): the version that pauses it feels fair and is the exact shape of the complaint *"they asked for a letter they weren't entitled to ask for, then treated the delay as ours"*. Ten days from HUD's FHEO-2020-01, deliberately not a `JurisdictionRule` — the FHA is federal, same reasoning as D-82. **Whether documentation may be requested is a hard refusal** (D-90) computed from the animal kind plus two observations recorded at intake and never recomputed: asking a service-animal handler is *itself* the violation, so the control is not rendered at all and there is no reason field to override with. **Denial grounds are prose prompts, never a picklist** (D-91) — a dropdown of denial reasons becomes a dropdown of things it is safe to say — and two CHECK constraints back it: a decided request needs date, author and written text or none of them, and an approval must name the animal. Intake raises a `Task` (D-9) and the determination is sent through the engine (R-030), email-only because a 160-character "approved" is not a written determination. **Found along the way:** a real defect the browser test caught and no unit test could — a React 19 form action resets its uncontrolled fields, so a refused determination emptied the written text; fixed with the `values` echo `recordLeaseNotice` already carries. **And a limitation behind it, measured rather than assumed:** the echo repopulates text fields and does nothing for a `<select>`, because React applies a select's `defaultValue` only at mount — so `SelectField` carries it for every caller and `lifecycle-panel.tsx`'s "Who gave notice" has the same latent instance, left for an item that can test the fallout of fixing it. Plus two accessible-name collisions of my own — "Determination" is a substring of "The written determination", and the panel's intro prose duplicated the clock's own closing line | PRD RISK-13; D-87, D-88, D-89, D-90, D-91 | M | R-052, R-063 | Phase 2 |
| 87 | ✅ R-087 | **The risk runs both ways, and a product guarding only one direction makes the other worse.** Act too soon and it is a self-help eviction plus conversion; act too late and a unit nobody has looked inside has a burst pipe, a pet, or a person who has died in it. So **nothing here declares a unit abandoned** — `assessEvidence` reports gaps and blocks nothing, and its 3-attempts-across-2-methods bar is labelled a **house rule, not a statute**, everywhere it surfaces (D-93). It counts DISTINCT methods, because three calls to a disconnected number is not trying, and a `REACHED` outcome is called out first and unconditionally. **The one hard refusal is disposal** (D-92), and it is also **the only place in this product where an unconfigured jurisdiction rule blocks rather than warns**: every other clock reports "not configured" and leaves the call with the human, but disposal is the only step that cannot be undone — somebody's photographs in a skip are gone, and conversion is a tort. `belongingsNoticeDays` is three-valued so a state with a notice rule cannot be satisfied by waiting alone. **Contact attempts are their own table, not `Message` rows** (D-94): half the methods send nothing at all — a door knock, a neighbour, a police welfare check — and those are the persuasive evidence. **The welfare-check entry reuses R-027's `entryDecision`** (D-95) because a welfare check is not a special kind of entry in any statute, and it warns rather than blocks, with the basis and rule id snapshotted onto the audit row. Four CHECK constraints refuse the indefensible records: a close with no account, an entry with no findings, a hold with no clock, a disposal with no hold. **Found along the way:** a fourth and fifth accessible-name collision in three items — the fifth latent in `shell.spec.ts` since R-080, where "Maintenance" also matches "Preventive maintenance", one function above a test that already used `exact: true` for exactly that reason (now a standing trap in CLAUDE.md, because the knowledge was already in the repo and did not travel), and **a latent cross-file race** — two unit-test files both seeded `state: 'ZZ'` and `rulesFor` reads every rule for a state, so one file's cap was masked by the other's rows; adding an unrelated core test file was enough to tip it over | PRD RISK-01; D-92, D-93, D-94, D-95 | M | R-083, R-027 | Phase 2 |
| 88 | ✅ R-088 | **There is no `HOARDING` value anywhere in this item, and its absence is the design** (**D-96**). You cannot serve a notice for hoarding: no lease term forbids it and no code section names it. What is enforceable is the blocked exit, the pest harborage, the furnace nobody can reach — and hoarding disorder is a recognised disability, so a case file headed with the word is both unenforceable AND the most quotable document in the complaint that follows. A hoarding case is a `PREMISES_CONDITION` case whose every observation names a `ViolationGround` from a closed list containing no word for a person, a diagnosis or a standard of housekeeping. **The enum IS the guardrail** — a free-text box is where "hoarder" gets typed — and a unit test plus an e2e assertion both fail if a later session adds one. **One `ViolationCase` table for all three situations**, because they are one shape: an alleged breach, dated observations with photographs, a notice series with a cure deadline, and four exits. Photos hang off the OBSERVATION, not the case: a hoarding file is a series of dated visits and a photo that cannot say which visit it came from proves the condition existed at some point, which is not what anybody is arguing about. **The legitimize exit runs the ordinary screening path with no shortcut** (**D-97**) — RISK-02 calls it the common real-world outcome, and the tempting build is a "manager approves the occupant" button, which is disparate treatment recorded in your own system with a name and a date on it. `legitimizedApplicantId` points at an `Applicant`, and closing is refused unless that applicant has a screening decision. A `PREMISES_CONDITION` case has **no** legitimize route at all: a blocked fire exit does not become permitted by agreement, and offering that outcome is how the agreement gets made. **`AccommodationRequest` was widened from assistance-animals-only to the general FHA framework** (**D-98**) rather than a second table beside it — a hoarding accommodation is never an animal, and two tables recording one statutory process is two vocabularies of which one rots. `AssistanceAnimalKind` became `AccommodationKind` with `POLICY_EXCEPTION`; the shape needed no change because the two observability questions were always the Joint Statement's general rule, not an animal rule. **One hard refusal** (**D-99**): an animal case will not close as "authorized pet" while an accommodation request on the tenancy is undecided, because that answers the request in the direction that costs the tenant money before anybody has decided it — and unlike every other warning here, the workaround is not to destroy a record, it is to decide the request, which was owed within ten days anyway. Escalating to eviction with a request open **warns and demands a reason**, per D-79. **Left behind:** no notice GENERATION from the case (notices are linked, not drafted — `LEASE_VIOLATION` already exists as a type and R-062's template path owns the text); no Texas seed for `leaseViolationCureDays`, so the cure clock correctly reports "not configured"; nothing raises a Task and nothing sweeps, so a stalled case is visible only to somebody who opens `/violations`; and an occupant's application still has to be created against a `Listing`, because `Application.listingId` is required and making it nullable is R-060 surgery this item did not do | PRD RISK-02, RISK-03 | M | R-060, R-073 | Phase 2 |
| 89 | ✅ R-089 | **`InsuranceClaim` has no `repairCostCents` column, and that absence is the shape of the whole item** (**D-100**). RISK-07 asks for "payout vs actual repair cost" and the obvious build is a figure typed off the adjuster's worksheet. D-19 settled it: a job's cost is typed ONCE, on the work order, and every downstream reader computes from that row — the backlog itself calls the WO → invoice → P&L chain "the specific place owners abandon software", and it breaks the same way every time. So a claim LINKS work orders (`WorkOrder.insuranceClaimId`) and `claimPosition` sums R-032e's existing `jobCostCents` over them; the claim's repair cost and the property's maintenance spend cannot disagree, and there is nowhere on the page to type a different number. The deductible and loss-of-rents cover are `InsurancePolicy`'s (R-015) for the same reason, which is why `policyId` is NOT NULL — a claim against no policy is one nobody can evaluate, and the panel refuses to render rather than pretending. **The rent in a loss-of-rents figure is never typed either**: the tenancy covering the start date is looked up and its contract rent used, falling back to the unit's asking rent, and **which source was used travels with the number** (**D-101**) because a contract rent is evidence and an asking rent is an assertion a carrier discounts. **`LedgerEntry` is strictly lease-scoped, so proceeds do not go through it** — they are a dedicated fact table read into `TaxExportFacts`, exactly as `EvictionCost` already is. **On Schedule E the two halves part company** (**D-102**): loss-of-rents proceeds replace rent and map to `RENTS_RECEIVED`; damage proceeds are EXCEPTED with the reasoning attached, never netted against the repair, because whether they reduce a deduction, reduce basis or defer under §1033 is the preparer's call and getting it wrong silently is worse than not making it. A claimed work order is deliberately **not** excluded from the expense side — a repair paid for by insurance is still a deductible repair, and a sixth exclusion door would delete the deduction *and* leave the proceeds unreported. `payment.category` is NOT NULL because it is knowable exactly once, while the cheque and its letter are in front of somebody. **Found along the way: Server Actions were capping every upload in the product at 1 MB** — inspections, notice service, abandonment, violations — against phone photos that are routinely 2–5 MB; raised to 25 MB in `next.config.ts`. Also fixed a latent teardown race in `listings.spec.ts`. **Left behind:** no long-video path (25 MB admits a short clip; a real one needs direct-to-storage upload and no item owns it); premiums still unsourced on the `INSURANCE` Schedule E line, unchanged by this item; nothing sweeps a stalled claim into a Task | PRD RISK-07 | M | R-032, R-078 | Phase 2 |
| 90 | ✅ R-090 | **The same `Lease` row is the whole item, and two of the four asks turned out to be things NOT to build** (**D-104**). A renewal creates a successor lease; a change of occupants must not — and with the row kept, "ledger continuity" stops being a feature (no lease ended, so no balance moved, no subscription cancelled, no deposit recharged) and "the deposit stays with the unit" stops being a rule to enforce (a disposition is triggered by a tenancy ENDING, and none did). So `LeasePartyChange` has **no money column of any kind**, which is how RISK-10's no-partial-refunds rule is actually made true: no field and no code path could pay one. **The screening record is a DATABASE CHECK, not a validation** (**D-105**) — `applicantId` is NOT NULL for an incoming party, so a replacement nobody screened cannot exist in the table, with no override, because the same-criteria-for-everybody defence is not ours to waive. **One document, signed by everybody including guarantors and the person leaving** (**D-106**): the release is a clause, and a release signed only by the person being released is its weakest form. Reuses R-063's entire ceremony via `LeaseEnvelope.kind`; `effectiveOn` is printed, never scheduled, because a job removing a roommate on a future date would cut their portal access and notices while they still live there. **Found along the way:** `removeLeaseTenant` would take somebody off a live, signed tenancy with one click — no release, no signatures, nothing told to the person leaving — refusing only when it would have emptied the lease | PRD RISK-10; D-104, D-105, D-106 | S | R-063, R-041 | Phase 2 |
| 91 | ✅ R-091 | **SPLIT AT THE START — this row is an L wearing an M.** Four asks, two of them whole legal processes. R-091 is the containment half: the case file, the permission wall and the lock change. **R-091b is early-termination rights and lease bifurcation**, below. **Nothing here names what it protects** (**D-107**): RISK-04 is domestic violence, and a table called `DvCase`, a URL reading `/dv/abc123` or an audit action `dv.case_opened` each disclose the exact fact the access control exists to hold — to anyone reading a schema, a browser history, a log line, or the screen over somebody's shoulder. Audit payloads carry the **case id and nothing else** (the closure reason excepted, deliberately), and **nothing raises a `Task`** because `Task.title` is read by everyone holding `task.read`. **No protective order is ever stored** (**D-108**) — only that documentation of a class was produced, by whom, on what date, because a `Document` row is readable by the maintenance tech; and documentation is never required to act, only to claim the statutory right. **The case is confidential; its consequences cannot be** (**D-109**): the re-key is an ordinary work order a dispatcher assigns, carrying a note that names **only who may be given keys**, never who may not. **Found along the way — a shipped access-control defect well outside this item: seven list pages are unreachable for any entity- or property-scoped staff user**, the R-007 resource-less-guard bug, still live. Filed as **R-103** | PRD RISK-04, ROLE-05; D-107, D-108, D-109 | M | R-084, R-063 | Phase 2 |
| 91b | ✅ R-091b | **THE MIRROR IMAGE OF R-085, AND THAT IS THE WHOLE ITEM** (**D-111**). D-82 hardcodes the SCRA because §§3931/3955 are federal and uniform; a survivor's right to leave early is **state law** (VAWA binds federally assisted housing, a private SFR portfolio answers to e.g. Tex. Prop. Code §92.016/§92.0161), so D-4 applies unchanged — three new `JurisdictionRule` columns, **three-valued**, because "nobody has reviewed this state" is a different claim from "this state grants no such right" and the wrong one refuses somebody a statutory right. Like §3955 it skips `noticePeriodCheck`, for D-82's reason arriving from a different statute. **Unlike §3955 it writes no basis column on the `Lease`**: `scraTerminationBasis` is safe because being a servicemember is not a secret, and a column naming THIS basis would be read by everybody holding `lease.read`. The tenancy shows an ordinary tenant-given notice; the claim lives behind the wall. **Bifurcation is one shared builder behind two entry points and never a "skip signatures" checkbox** (**D-112**) — the ordinary panel has no parameter that could pass one — plus an enum, not free text, recording that a statute excused the signature and never which statute. Found along the way: a checkbox label containing the word "state" broke `getByLabel('State')` in four existing specs | PRD RISK-04; D-4, D-82, D-104, D-107 | M | R-091, R-090 | Phase 2 |
| 103 | ✅ R-103 | **Seven list pages are unreachable for any scoped staff user — the R-007 resource-less-guard bug, still shipped.** `requirePermission('x.read')` with no resource only ever matches a PORTFOLIO-WIDE grant (`assignmentCovers` compares `resource.legalEntityId === assignment.legalEntityId`, and an absent resource gives `undefined`), so an entity- or property-scoped actor is sent to `/no-access` on a page whose scoped query would have shown them a perfectly real list. `requireScope` exists precisely for this and its own comment documents the bug — R-008 fixed Properties and Leases and the rest were never swept. **Confirmed empirically**, not inferred: an entity-scoped manager is bounced from `/violations`, `/evictions`, `/workorders`, `/money`, `/abandonment`, `/claims` and `/search`, while `/leases` and `/properties` work. Roughly twenty more call sites in `lib/*/actions.ts` have the same shape and each needs the same judgement — genuinely portfolio-wide (jurisdiction rules, message and document templates) or wrongly resource-less. **A lockout, not a leak**, so nobody is exposed; it makes the product unusable for every non-owner scope. Found by R-091, whose own two pages and one action shipped with it before an e2e scoping test caught them. **Fixed at 21 call sites across 18 files, and the rule is now a test** (**D-110**): `route-guards.test.ts` fails the build on any resource-less `requirePermission` under `app/` or `lib/` unless the file is on `RESOURCE_LESS_GUARDS` with a written reason — the same reviewable-line shape `PUBLIC_ROUTES` uses, proven by making it fail. The 26 exemptions are the genuinely portfolio-wide ones (jurisdiction rules, message/document/inspection templates, vendors, entity creation); the preventive-maintenance trio is labelled the weakest of them, because splitting viewing from editing is the real answer and widening access unasked is the dangerous direction | PRD ROLE-01, ROLE-04; D-5, D-110 | M | R-008 | MVP |
| 92 | ✅ R-092 | **AN EXPORT IS WHERE EVERY ACCESS-CONTROL DECISION IN THE PRODUCT GETS RE-ASKED AT ONCE** (**D-113**), so what the packet leaves out is as designed as what it carries: access codes inventoried but never printed (R-005 made every reveal individually audited; a bulk print collapses all of them into one log line, in a file that gets forwarded), no mortgage or insurance declarations (neither transfers with the house), and nothing from a confidential case — though the re-key job R-091 raises IS in the vendor history, because D-109 says a case's consequences cannot be hidden, while `restrictedPartyNote` is not. Enforced by a **source-level test** in R-103's shape, because the packet's own output can never catch a query that starts selecting one more column. New `property.export`, **owner-only by construction and privileged** — the only read on that list besides `accesscode.reveal`, and the exception holds because refusing it gates one button and no screen. **The three honesty rules** (**D-114**): the estoppel states the LANDLORD's figures and asks the tenant to correct them, the deposit-transfer notice is a form with visible blanks rather than a merge template with an invented buyer in it, and the exhibit index names every tenancy with no certificate rather than shipping a packet that looks complete | PRD RISK-09, DOC-06; D-107, D-109, D-50 | M | R-062, R-015 | Phase 2 |


> **Demo checkpoint 3 (Golden Path 3 — the confidential path).** Defined 2026-08-24, when Milestone 8 closed with **no checkpoint of its own** — twelve risk items shipped without an end-to-end gate, which is precisely the condition D-28 exists to prevent.
>
> An owner opens a confidential safety case on a tenancy with two occupants → names the co-tenant as the restricted party → orders the re-key, which raises an ordinary urgent work order carrying a note that names only who MAY receive keys, retires every access code on file, revokes **both** occupants' smart-lock door codes at the device and hands the survivor a replacement, shown once → sends the lease amendment that removes the restricted party without their signature, which the survivor signs and the restricted party is never asked to → records the statutory early termination, which appears on the tenancy as an ordinary tenant-given notice with a computed date and no basis column → and then a **manager** and the **property handoff packet** and the **staff calendar feed** are each checked for what they can see.
>
> **The gate is what the other five surfaces show.** The manager sees the re-key job and the amendment and nothing else. The handoff packet lists the job and never the restricted-party note. The calendar carries the visit and never the note. The audit trail carries the case id and never the summary. The lease page shows a tenant-given notice and never a reason. Every one of those is a different item's seam, and each item's own tests were written from inside it.
>
> **Why this path and not another.** It is the only one that crosses six items shipped across three sessions — R-091, R-091b, R-091c, R-094b, R-092, R-097c — every one of which independently promises that something does not leak. D-28's four original findings were all seam defects of exactly this shape: a status added to an enum and not to the lists that read it, a lifecycle ending in one table and not the other. No amount of per-item testing finds those.

## Milestone 9: Phase 3 — depth, real vendors & reach

| # | ID | Item | PRD/Feature | Size | Depends on | Phase |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 93 | R-093 | Real vendor drivers replacing the simulators (D-7): screening provider (partner + FCRA agreement), e-sign provider, listing syndication feeds, certified-mail API, QuickBooks Online API sync. Each slots behind its existing adapter interface | D-7; PRD §7 | L | R-060, R-063, R-057, R-078 | Phase 3 |
| 94 | ✅ R-094 | **SPLIT AT THE START — the row is two features for two different people.** R-094 is the self-showing: identity verification, the one-time code, the window, the entry log and the instant kill, for a **stranger who saw a listing**. R-094b below is the smart-lock code lifecycle tied to lease state, which is about **tenants**. **Every rule here follows from who is being let in** (**D-115**): a vacant unit always (an unaccompanied code on an occupied home is a stranger with a key to somebody's house), the window and not the link is the control, the code lives on a page and in no message, `ShowingAccess.identityCheckId` is NOT NULL at the database, and **no photo ID is stored anywhere** — D-108's reasoning applied to a stranger. **The name comparison is ours, not the provider's** (**D-116**): a real licence belonging to somebody else comes back VERIFIED from any provider, and only this system knows who booked the slot; D-27 is what makes the simulator answer with a name it was TOLD, or the mismatch branch is dead code. **The kill is the cheap path and the DEVICE has to hear it** (**D-117**) — our page refusing to show a code is only the second lock. Found along the way: the booking confirmation promised "a member of our team will meet you there" to somebody nobody was meeting; and a warning asking staff to go and change a lock was destroyed by its own re-render, which is why `revokeReachedDevice` is a column | PRD LEASE-08, PROP-03; D-27, D-108, D-15 | M | R-064 | Phase 3 |
| 94b | ✅ R-094b | **ONE CODE PER PERSON, AND THE ASYMMETRY IS THE DESIGN** (**D-118**): a shared household code makes both of this feature's purposes impossible — the log cannot say who came in, and a roommate leaving cannot come off the door without changing everybody's code. Issuing is deliberate and keeps R-069's two hard gates (funds cleared, no hold halting access); revoking has no gate, is not privileged, and happens **on its own** when the tenancy leaves force and when somebody comes off the lease. **No `validTo`**, unlike a showing's window: an expiring tenant code is a paying tenant locked out of their own home at midnight because a fixed term rolled to MTM. **The two directions fail in opposite ways** (**D-119**): a device that refuses an ISSUE writes no row at all (a record of a code the door never heard of gets read out to a tenant), while a device that refuses a REVOKE gets the row anyway with `revokeReachedDevice = false`, in red, until somebody drives out. Killing the portal session — all R-090 could do — was always the smaller half | PRD PROP-03, LEASE-08; D-115, D-117, D-84 | M | R-094, R-069 | Phase 3 |
| 91c | ✅ R-091c | **The safety gap R-094b named and nothing owned.** R-091's re-key retires `AccessCode` rows and sends a locksmith, and its own panel says retiring changes no lock — true, and the exception is the one that matters: a smart-lock door code IS the lock, so a restricted party kept working access to the home until the locksmith physically arrived. Now the re-key revokes **every** door code on the unit at the device (households share codes, so the restricted party walks in on the survivor's) and immediately mints a replacement for everybody still authorized, shown once on the case page — built from the same expression as the locksmith's authorized-names note, so the two lists can never disagree (**D-120**). No funds or hold gate on the re-issue: those guard a move-in, and refusing a sitting tenant her code back because a deposit has not cleared gets the situation backwards. Found along the way: two different events were sharing `confidential.codes_retired` with two payload shapes | PRD RISK-04, PROP-03; D-107, D-109, D-118 | S | R-091, R-094b | Phase 3 |

Cut rather than deferred, on D-121's reasoning and R-096's precedent: a row nobody will build reads as work waiting to be done. **The sole owner is already served, which is why this costs nothing** — R-081a's operating snapshot and R-081b/d's tax packet are per legal entity, and `read_only` has existed since R-004, so "show one LLC's numbers without letting them touch anything" works today. **What survives is entity scoping itself** (D-123 made it the load-bearing scope in this portfolio), which is what an investor role would have been built on. **Re-opens on one fact**: the first entity that takes on a partner with a distribution right — at which point the work is the contribution/distribution ledger and the ownership-split model, not the read-only access | PRD ROLE-04, RPT-08; D-133 | M | R-004, R-081a | ~~Phase 3~~ cut |

Cut rather than deferred, on D-121's reasoning: a row nobody will build reads as work waiting to be done. **The machinery underneath survives** — R-049's translation templates and their `template.approve` gate stay, unused, because that is the expensive half to add back under pressure. **Re-opens on one fact**: the first household whose primary language is not English, at which point the work is writing and reviewing notice translations, not building a localization system | PRD §6.4; D-10, D-122 | M | R-049, R-018 | ~~Phase 3~~ cut |
| 97 | R-097 | **SPLIT INTO THREE, AND TWO OF THE FIVE THINGS IN IT WERE CUT RATHER THAN SIZED** (2026-08-24; **D-121**). The row bundled five deliverables that share no query surface, no persona and no gate. Three became rows below. **The native tech app is cut**: it is gated on R-028's field data, R-028 is gated on there being an in-house tech, and D-17 already answered that there is not one — a row whose premise a decision removed is worse than no row, because it reads as buildable. **MAINT-13's v1 already shipped**: the PRD's own text is "v1 marks paid; money moves outside", and R-042's vendor invoices record `paidAt` and `paymentMethod` today. What is left of MAINT-13 is real money movement, which the PRD scores **[W]** and which needs a licensed rail, so it stays unbuilt on purpose rather than sitting in a bundle | PRD PAY-15, COMM-08, MAINT-13 | ~~XL~~ split | R-093 | Phase 3 |
| 97a | ✅ R-097a | **The routing decision was already built, and reusing it whole is the item** (**D-125**). `decideRoute` refuses to guess between candidates and explains why at length; `candidatesForEmail` hands it the same shape, so a tenant live at two properties refuses by email exactly as by phone. **Email adds two things SMS does not.** A **reply key** in the address (`hello+<key>@inbound…`) names the THREAD, which is the only high-confidence match there is — it buys **precision, not trust**, resolving the AMBIGUOUS case From: matching cannot, and a key naming no thread falls through rather than failing. `Thread.replyKey` is plaintext where every `AuthToken` is hashed, because it must be rendered into a Reply-To and authenticates nobody: an inbound From: header is exactly as weak as the caller ID R-017 already lives with. And a **quoted tail** — `Message` is append-only, so without stripping, the third reply stores the first two again for ever. **Subject lines are never matched on**, which is the commonest way this feature leaks | PRD COMM-08; D-7, D-9, D-125 | M | R-032 | Phase 3 |
| 97d | ✅ R-097d | **The defect R-097a left behind, fixed in both halves** (**D-126**). It filed the words and threw away the photograph — silently — and stored nothing at all for an HTML-only message, which several mobile clients send by default. Attachments now become `Document` rows hung off the message they arrived on, under the rules an unauthenticated sender demands: a **closed** type list rather than a block list, a size cap and a count cap, the declared type recorded but never trusted, and the filename sanitised for display as well as for the storage key. The HTML converter adds no dependency and only ever DELETES, leaving unrecognised entities as written — a visible `&hellip;` is a blemish, a wrong guess is a changed sentence in an evidence trail. **And the unrouted half is fixed too**: a Document must have a property and an unrouted message has none, so the COUNT is recorded and triage is told a photograph was sent and could not be kept. Fixing only the easy half would have left the same defect in the harder one | PRD COMM-08; D-125, DOC-05 | M | R-097a, R-019 | Phase 3 |
| 97e | ✅ R-097e | **The last gap R-097a/d left: email honoured no opt-out** while SMS has honoured `STOP` since R-040e (**D-127**). An email opt-out is **not** an SMS `STOP` — that one is a single token the carrier enforces, this is prose nobody else acts on — so the false-positive trade-off **inverts**: `classifyOptOutKeyword` is deliberately generous, and here a tenant whose rent reminders were silently muted because they wrote "stop" about a dripping tap gets a late fee. Every phrase is multi-word, `stop` alone is not one, and detection reads the STRIPPED body, since every marketing footer carries "unsubscribe". **The dangerous outcome is not that we kept emailing them**: four categories are locked on by NOTIF-02, so the failure to avoid is a tenant who believes they switched everything off, misses an entry notice, and finds somebody in their home. Hence one more email, naming what still arrives in `LOCKED_CATEGORIES`' own words, sent outside the preference engine so the announcement cannot be suppressed by the change it announces | PRD COMM-08, NOTIF-02; D-127 | S | R-097a, R-030 | Phase 3 |
| 97f | ✅ R-097f | **SMS opened maintenance tickets and email opened nothing** — somebody emailing "my boiler is broken" got a conversation and no work queue item (**D-128**). R-021's intake shape verbatim, including the ordering that matters: thread first, ticket second, because a message nobody could route must never open one. **The one real difference is that email is a conversation** — nobody texts to say "thanks", so R-021's rule is right for SMS and would drown R-023's triage queue here. Drawn on **evidence, never on what the words look like**: a reply key is proof, recent outbound is good enough when a mail system stripped the tag, and guessing "is this a complaint?" from prose is the same mistake `formatSmsTicketDescription` refuses one level down. An opt-out is not a repair either, so R-097e's check comes first. **And the photograph is hung off the ticket**, not only the message — without it the person dispatched to fix the leak never sees the picture | PRD COMM-08, MAINT-01; D-125, D-128 | M | R-097d, R-021 | Phase 3 |
| 97b | R-097b | **Rent credit-bureau reporting opt-in** (PAY-15). **GATED ON A VENDOR AND ON THE REVIEW D-4 ALREADY REQUIRES, and the second gate is the real one.** Furnishing payment data to a consumer reporting agency makes this product a **furnisher** under 15 U.S.C. §1681s-2, with accuracy duties and an obligation to investigate disputes — the same class of obligation R-061's adverse-action path already answers to, and not something to discover after the first tenant disputes a late mark. What is ours whatever vendor is chosen, and is most of the work: **the consent record** (who opted in, when, to what wording, provable later), **an opt-out that actually stops furnishing** rather than only flipping a flag, and **what is furnished** — a decision, not a dump, since a ledger built from Stripe carries charges a bureau has no business seeing. Third parties are Esusu, Rent Reporters, Boom; each needs a contract and a furnishing agreement. **Do not build a simulated driver** (D-15's rule): a simulator that mints fake bureau submissions is untested code that looks finished, against a real person's credit file | PRD PAY-15; D-4, D-15 | M | vendor decision, furnisher review | Phase 3 |
| 97c | ✅ R-097c | **The story got written first (NOTIF-06), then the smaller half got built** — and the row's own instruction turned out to be right twice over. **An .ics subscription, not a calendar vendor's API** (**D-124**): RFC 5545 is a feature Google, Outlook and iCloud all already have, so one URL replaces three OAuth integrations — and it **makes two-way impossible by construction**, which is the safe direction, since this row's own warning was that a wrong conflict policy silently moves a legally-noticed entry appointment (R-027). **What goes into a third party's calendar is a disclosure decision**: where, when and what KIND of visit, never a tenant's name or contact details, never `restrictedPartyNote` (though the job appears — D-109), and never a work-order's free-text scope, because a scope line can say anything about a household and a closed vocabulary of visit kinds cannot. Source-level test, R-092's shape. Scope resolved on every fetch rather than frozen into the token, so deactivation kills the feed on the next poll | PRD NOTIF-06; D-107, D-109, D-124 | M | R-064, R-027 | Phase 3 |

> **Demo checkpoint 4 (Golden Path 4 — the stranger's path).** Defined 2026-08-24, and defined **before** anybody claimed Milestone 9 was closed, which is the half checkpoint 3 had to learn the hard way. R-097f's own PROGRESS entry already said Milestone 9 had no checkpoint and that whoever closed it should define one first.
>
> **The thesis, and why it is a different question from checkpoint 3's.** Milestone 9 built two surfaces the OUTSIDE WORLD drives and neither of which can authenticate anybody: a smart-lockbox self-showing (R-094, R-094b) and inbound email (R-097a, R-097d, R-097e, R-097f). A stranger types a name into a page; a stranger sends a `From:` header. Both are as trustworthy as caller ID, and both are allowed to cause real things — a door opening, a maintenance ticket, a permanent line in somebody's evidence trail. So the walk asks **what an unauthenticated stranger is allowed to cause**, where checkpoint 3 asked what leaks.
>
> One person, followed the whole way. A stranger books a viewing on a vacant house → is refused when the name on the ID is not the name that booked it → is let in when it is, with the code on a page and in no message and no photo ID stored anywhere → the owner pulls the code and her next refresh loses it → **she moves in** → the same unit now refuses a self-showing outright, because somebody is living there → she is given her own door code, one per person, and the audit records that the door was programmed and never the code → she **emails** about a leaking boiler with a photograph attached, which threads, opens a ticket and hangs the photograph off the ticket → she emails again asking to be unsubscribed from everything, which switches off every email she is allowed to switch off and opens no second ticket.
>
> **The gate is the visit her own email caused.** The work order is scheduled, and the entry notice goes to the address she just asked us to stop using — because a locked category outranks any opt-out, and the outcome R-097e's own header names is a tenant who believes she switched everything off and finds somebody in her home. Then the job is marked complete, and the "did we fix it?" follow-up is `SUPPRESSED / preference_off`. **Both halves or neither proves anything:** if everything survived an opt-out the opt-out would not work, and the contrast is what tells them apart. R-027 and R-097e were built four milestones apart and have never met anywhere else.
>
> **What it found was in the product** (D-132), unlike checkpoint 3: `From: Name <addr@example.com>` — the form every real mail client sends — never routed at all.
>
> **Its remaining half was closed 2026-08-24** (D-135): `To:`/`Cc:` are genuinely lists, and splitting them on every comma cost the reply key whenever another recipient's display name contained one. `splitAddressList` is quote-aware and sits beside `emailAddressOnly`, so both halves of header parsing are one file.

> **Demo checkpoint 5 (Golden Path 5 — the money path).** Defined and walked 2026-08-24. Checkpoint 3's PROGRESS entry and checkpoint 4's both end with the same note — the money path has no equivalent walk, and R-083's evictions, R-084's holds and R-087's abandonment make promises of the same kind about what automation must not do to a protected tenancy. This is that walk, at the third time of asking, and it is the checkpoint Milestone 8 never had for its **collections** half (checkpoint 3 covered its confidential half).
>
> **The thesis, and it is neither of theirs.** Checkpoint 3 asked what leaks; checkpoint 4 asked what an unauthenticated stranger is allowed to cause. This one asks: **when the law puts a fence around a tenancy, what must the collections machine stop doing — and what must the fence never forgive?** R-044 built the arrears chase in Milestone 4; R-084 built the fence in Milestone 8, four milestones later; R-085 built the one statute that raises it by itself. They meet nowhere in the suite. `lib/holds/holds.test.ts` proves `leasesHalted` returns the right **set**, `scra.spec.ts` proves a certificate places the hold, `rent-roll.spec.ts` proves the chase chases — and nothing joins the three, which is where an operator actually lives.
>
> One tenancy, followed as its legal status changes underneath a screen somebody is already looking at. She falls behind → is chased, lawfully and correctly, asserted at the notification engine rather than the screen → the rent roll is re-rendered, she is ticked, the template chosen, and **nothing is pressed** → in a second session a DMDC certificate comes back `in_service` and the SCRA hold places itself, the only automatic hold in the product → **then** the aimed press lands.
>
> **The gate is that press, and it has two halves that must both hold.** It must refuse — R-084's own text claims exactly this, that a hold placed while the screen was open stops the chase the same way a payment does — and it must say **why** it refused, because under a federal protection the reason a chase was withheld is the record. Then the screen is reloaded: the chase is no longer offered and the row says so, **and the balance is still $1,500.00 in its late bucket**. If the debt had vanished with the chase the product would have forgiven something nobody decided to forgive; if the chase had survived it would have violated the stay while displaying a banner saying it was in force. Only the contrast tells a guard from an off switch.
>
> **What it found was in the product** (D-134), as checkpoint 4's was: the refusal blamed the grace period. A tenancy under an automatic stay was reported to the operator, and written into the audit row, as *"not past the grace period"* — and the rent roll went on offering the checkbox and counting it in "select all past grace".


## Milestone 10: demo readiness

The product is built and the backlog above is closed. What is missing is the ability to **show** it: the demo seed is still R-013's walking skeleton — legal entities, properties, units, tenants, leases and a single overdue charge — so roughly eighty items' worth of screens open empty. Added 2026-08-25 by owner decision after asking whether the product could give full demos; the answer was that any single flow demos live, but the app cannot be opened and shown as a working business.

**Split into three at the start rather than halfway** (the L-splitting rule), and the split falls out of one constraint discovered while scoping: **D-11 forbids seeding the ledger.** `LedgerEntry` is a Stripe-webhook projection and a row Stripe does not know about is a reconciliation bug, so the money story cannot be written directly the way the maintenance and leasing stories can. That makes the money slice a different kind of problem from the other two, and it goes last rather than first.

|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 100a | ✅ R-100a | **The maintenance and vendor story, and the reset really was the work** (**D-143**). `MAINTENANCE`, a map keyed `"<property>::<unit>"` merged into `buildPlan()`: five tickets across NEW / WAITING_ON_TENANT / CONVERTED / CLOSED, four work orders (assigned, scheduled, complete-with-invoice, and one preventive), three vendors, three PM schedules, an inspection due with unanswered items and one performed and locked, plus eight messages on real `Thread`s keyed by core's own `threadKey`. **One live vendor magic link, printed once** - the raw token exists only in that moment, since only its SHA-256 is stored. *The reset*: `Message` is append-only with `onDelete: Restrict`, so the old bottom-up delete now throws partway, having already removed the rows above. The decision moved UP to the property - deleted whole or retired whole, never unpicked - with stickiness = any AuditLog or any Thread carrying messages. Verified by running seed + three resets from a clean database: six active properties every time, and live vendor tokens holding steady at one because retiring a job now revokes its link. *Found on the way*: an `INVOICED` work order is invisible - `listOpenWorkOrders` filters to a list that excludes it - so the status list moved to core as `OPEN_WORK_ORDER_STATUSES` and `demo-seed.test.ts` now asserts the seed only uses statuses a screen renders | PRD §8; R-013 | M | R-020, R-025, R-032 | Demo |

Five tenancies, one per lifecycle: square on autopay, mid-chase and declined, a part-payment on `send_invoice`, an ACH still in flight, and a decline-then-paid. `reset()` gained two sticky sources (`LedgerEntry`, `Notification`), cancels the retired tenancy's subscription, and unpublishes its listing | PRD §8; D-11, D-12, D-145, D-146 | M | R-034, R-035, R-100a | Demo |

No 404s, no 500s, no crashes - and **seven defects**, three of them in the product rather than the demo. **The product ones**: an eviction case reported "Notice not yet served" while displaying the served notice, and could never be filed (**D-148**, owner-decided, both notice types now start the cure clock); **every move-in proration charge ever written has no audit row at all** - `ledger.adjusted` is on REASON_REQUIRED and the call passed no reason, so `recordAudit` threw into a swallowing `.catch` and the only symptom was a console line in a seed log; and `/leases/[id]/deposit` rendered its empty state with no `h1`. **The environment one**: `npm run dev` and `npm run start` had no `-p`, so this repo's dev server landed on **:3000** - the sibling self-storage app's port, and the exact collision the port table exists to prevent. `playwright.config.ts` owning 3100 never covered the bare scripts. **The demo ones**: `/tasks` - "My day", the screen a PM opens first and D-9's only queue - was completely empty; `/money` reported "Never synced" for all five payers the seed had just provisioned; and `/inspections/new` **refused to work at all** ("No checklists yet. Build one first") with two walked inspections on the list behind it. *What that says about the seed*: three of the four demo gaps were screens the seed had written rows AROUND rather than THROUGH - inspections built from inline item plans and never from the checklist a human would have had to build first. | 103 | ✅ R-106 | **A failed delivery is not a finished delivery** (R-104's two leftovers, chosen 2026-08-25 by owner decision after R-105 closed the demo walk). Two halves, both about not losing a message silently, and they are one item because they meet in the same function. **(a) No retry policy.** A `FAILED` delivery stays failed and nothing re-queues it. `failureCode` was built to be branched on and **nothing branches on it** - written in four places, rendered in three, read for a decision in none - so a rate limit and a dead address are treated identically and both are permanent. **(b) A synchronous `21610` records no opt-out.** When Twilio refuses the API call outright because the recipient has sent STOP, no message exists and no status callback ever arrives, so the carrier opt-out list (R-054) never learns what the send just discovered and the next attempt fails the same way. **Three things already known, so they are not re-derived.** (1) `isOptOutErrorCode` in `packages/core/comms/delivery-status.ts` already tests exactly these codes, and R-104 made both drivers return the provider's own code, so the *test* exists and only the branch is missing. (2) The branch belongs in **`deliver.ts`'s single `catch`, where FAILED is written for every channel** - not in the Twilio driver, which would fix one adapter and leave the next one to rediscover it. (3) `SmsOptOutSource.CARRIER_CALLBACK`'s own schema comment already reads *"The provider told us a send was blocked - Twilio error 21610 and its relatives"*, which arguably covers a synchronous rejection as written - **decide whether that is honest enough or whether a third value is owed, and say which in PROGRESS**; a new enum value is a migration and CLAUDE.md's "adding a value to a status enum is never one edit" applies to both lists that read it. **What retry must not become**: a sweep that re-sends a `notice` or an `entry_notice` on its own is a legally operative act repeated without anybody deciding to - so the retryable set is a decision, not a default, and a permanent failure (bad address, opt-out, unsupported channel) must never be retried at all | PRD §7; R-030, R-054, D-15, D-147 | S | R-104 | Demo |

## Milestone 11: the accessibility debt

WCAG 2.1 AA is an acceptance criterion on tenant- and vendor-facing work in this repo and has been since PRD §6 — *"not a later cleanup"*. It was never audited by anything reading for it. On 2026-08-25 five agents did, across all 88 routes: `docs/audits/2026-08-25-accessibility-ux.md`, 75 findings.

**Split into two at the start** (the L-splitting rule), **and it ended up as eleven** — corrected here at R-119 rather than left reading as a plan that held. The original split was by *shape*: three of the findings are single edits in shared code that reach every screen in the product, and one is a mechanical sweep over 37 independent call sites, and mixing those would have produced one commit nobody could review. What the two halves did not anticipate is that the audit's own findings kept splitting further — R-107a and R-107b were the plan; R-108, R-109 and R-110 are defects the verification of those two turned up; R-111 through R-116 are the audit's remaining angles taken a persona at a time; R-117 is the demo walk D-28 owes at the close, and R-118 the money defect that walk found. Eleven rows, one milestone, and the count is the finding: **verifying an accessibility fix is where the next defect comes from**, so a sweep of this shape should be sized as a milestone rather than as an item with a split.

| # | ID | Item | PRD/Feature | Size | Depends on | Phase |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 104a | ✅ R-107a | **The shared layer: focus you can see, and focus that survives a submit** (audit items 1–3 and 10, chosen 2026-08-25). **(a) `--ring` at 2.59:1 and `--input` at 1.26:1** against white, both under 1.4.11's 3:1 — every field boundary and every focus ring in the product, and every control also sets `focus-visible:outline-none` so the ring is the *only* indicator. **(b) `SubmitButton` used `disabled={pending}`**, and a focused element that becomes `disabled` is blurred by the browser: ~108 forms threw keyboard focus to `<body>` on every press, silently, since the button also left the accessibility tree and the swap to "Working…" was announced to nobody. **(c) Thirteen buttons drew a focus ring at exactly 1.00:1** — Tailwind v4 defaults the ring to `currentcolor`, which on a `text-background` button *is* the background. **(d) `CheckboxField` set no focus classes at all**, falling back to a base `outline-ring/50` rule computing to 1.54:1 | PRD §6; WCAG 1.4.11, 2.4.7, 4.1.3 | M | R-106 | MVP |
| 104c | ✅ R-108 | **The constraint that was never going to fire.** Filed undiagnosed on purpose, and **the row's own premise was wrong** - so was R-107a's when it fixed the same shape in `renewals.spec.ts`. `@@unique([state, jurisdiction, version])` refuses nothing here: every fixture rule is statewide, `jurisdiction` is NULL, and **Postgres treats NULLs as DISTINCT**. Proven with SQL, not reasoned about - two `('QQ', NULL, 1)` rows insert in one transaction, `INSERT 0 2`. A repeat draw therefore creates a SECOND rule; `rulesFor` fetches both and `selectApplicableRule` breaks the `effectiveFrom` tie by keeping whichever row `findMany` returned first, with no `orderBy` anywhere. Nothing throws - **that is the gap the row demanded be explained**: the page renders the other branch, correctly, from another test's numbers. `null` inherited as `30` loses the 'not configured' message (15s `expect` timeout); `30` inherited as `null` never renders the disposal form, so `fill()` waits out the full **60s test timeout**, which is the symptom that was recorded. The fourth test reads no storage period, which is why it never failed. **Proven by making it fail**: the code pinned to a constant gives **3 failed, 5 passed, zero unique-constraint errors**, two of them at 1.0m - and both gates passed in the other browser project in the same run, which is the race showing itself. Fixed with `uniqueStateCode()` in `e2e/fixtures.ts`, genuinely unique rather than sequential - deliberately the opposite of `uniquePhone`'s rule, because a state code has no length constraint to fit inside and so there is nothing to gain by gambling. Green at `--repeat-each=3 --retries=0`, 24 of 24. **Left behind**: three specs still use fixed codes and still write duplicate rows, safe only because each always seeds the same body under a given code - and closing it at the database (`UNIQUE NULLS NOT DISTINCT`) would break all three and report schema drift. **Two unrelated suite bugs found on the way, filed as R-109** | test suite | S | — | MVP |
