## Milestone 15: Arc 5 — the money numbers are computed off the right base

Sourced from the 2026-09-13 operator review (`docs/reviews/2026-09-13-operator-review.md`), commissioned by D-222 after Arc 4 closed at R-204. Ordering: the **ten** findings where the product's behaviour is wrong come first, then the rest by operational pain × frequency, matching the review's own ranking. Four rows are **Needs counsel** and say so. The theme, in the review's words: Arc 3's defects were automated decisions that were silently wrong, and **Arc 4's — found now — are the money numbers themselves being computed off the wrong base, everywhere the ordinary monthly rent is involved**. D-11/D-40 decided the subscription's rent line mints no `Charge` row; three separate readers were then written against the `Charge` table as if it were the debt list, and all three are wrong in the normal case. R-205 and R-206 share that root and should be built in order, by the same session if it can hold them.

| # | ID | Item | PRD/Feature | Size | Depends on | Phase |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 192 | ✅ R-205 | **WRONG — a mid-month move-in is never assessed a late fee at all, and a first-of-month one is assessed a percentage of the whole arrears.** One root, two halves, provable from four lines. `LATE_FEE_APPLIES_TO = ['RENT']` (`apps/web/lib/ledger/late-fees.ts:68`); pass 2 — the pass that exists precisely because ordinary rent mints no charge row — excludes any lease holding one (`:306`), and `chargeMoveInProration` writes `type: 'RENT'` at activation (`apps/web/lib/billing/proration.ts:107-121`, called from `provision.ts:220`). So **any lease that did not start on its rent due day is excluded from pass 2 for ever**, pass 1 reaches only the proration, finds it paid and `continue`s (`:245`), and the tenancy's ordinary rent is assessed by neither pass — a tenant fifteen days late every month for three years is charged nothing while `/jobs` records `ledger.late_fees` SUCCEEDED with `chargesAssessed: 0`. Mirror half: the leases that *do* reach pass 2 get `outstandingCents: delinquency.balanceCents` — the whole ledger balance (`:352-357`) — so `PERCENT_OF_RENT` computes 5% of $3,000 = **$150** for being late on one $1,500 month, and at three months takes 5% of a figure that includes previously assessed fees. **Needs counsel** on whether a percentage fee may lawfully take arrears rather than the period's rent as its base; either way the two passes must not disagree, and today they do. Fix: derive the rent-period debts from the dated `CHARGE` `LedgerEntry` rows R-206 also needs, assess per period, and make `monthlyRentCents` the base for a percentage. **Do not backfill** the fees never assessed — report them. Review finding 1 | PRD PAY-04; D-4/D-12; R-040/R-042/R-118; review §1 | M | R-118 | Arc 5 |
| 193 | ✅ R-206 | **WRONG — on the day a second month of rent goes unpaid the aging anchor jumps to the oldest charge on file, and the chase ladder goes permanently silent.** `oldestUnsettled` builds the debt list as the `Charge` rows plus *one* month's rent at `nearestRentDueOn`, allocates newest-first, and falls back to `newestFirst[last].dueOn` when the balance outruns the list (`packages/core/ledger/aging.ts:221-238`) — which is exactly what two unpaid months means. Worked: move-in 15 Jul 2025 with a $726 proration paid on time, rent $1,500, nothing paid since February; at 20 May 2026 the balance is $4,500, the anchor becomes **2025-07-15**, and `daysLate` reads **309** instead of 49. That is the R-118 defect returning through the fallback branch, on the tenancy that matters most. Three consequences: the rent roll prints *"1 Jul 2025"* as the oldest unpaid date (`apps/web/lib/payments/rent-roll.ts:231-249`) — the date somebody reads out in a hearing; the report sorts by `daysLate` so it pins above genuinely older arrears; and `chaseRungDue` matches days past grace **exactly** against `[1, 5, 15]` (`aging.ts:340-344`), so at 309 days R-179's whole ladder fires **zero** times. The opposite case is equally wrong — a lease with no charge history reports two unpaid months as 19 days late, emptying the `30+` bucket on a portfolio full of 60-day arrears. **No schema change needed**: `invoice.finalized` already projects one dated `CHARGE` entry per period (`packages/core/billing/events.ts:293-318`, `apps/web/lib/billing/webhook.ts:340-355`) and `rentRoll` already loads them (`rent-roll.ts:155-163`). Age from those. Mapping `occurredAt` (the finalize instant) to the period's due date needs care and must be written down. Review finding 2 | PRD PAY-06/RPT-02; R-044/R-118/R-179; D-189; review §2 | M | R-205 | Arc 5 |
| 194 | ✅ R-207 | **WRONG — an emergency vendor dispatch between 9pm and 8am is held until morning, and the screen says it went.** `EMERGENCY_CATEGORIES` has exactly one member, `maintenance_emergency` (`packages/core/notifications/categories.ts:302-307`). The vendor dispatch sends on `work_order_assigned` with a `propertyId` (`apps/web/lib/workorders/actions.ts:407-422`), so it gets `deferUntil = quietHoursEndAfter(...)` and status `DEFERRED` (`apps/web/lib/notifications/send.ts:180-193,307-309`), and the drain only picks up `sendAfter: { lte: now }` (`:605`) — so the immediate `dispatchPendingNotifications` right after cannot send it. Default quiet hours are 21:00–08:00 (`packages/core/notifications/quiet-hours.ts:32`): **eleven hours**. The end-to-end scenario is the one this product was built for — tenant reports a sewage backup at 22:40, `maintenance_emergency` correctly pages the on-call PM, the PM assigns the 24-hour plumber, the page returns its success notice, **and the plumber's text is scheduled for 08:00**. Nothing on the screen says so, and `priority: 'EMERGENCY'` is already in the notify context, unread. `reissue.ts:105` has the same shape. Fix: bypass quiet hours when the work order's priority is EMERGENCY — or when the recipient is a VENDOR at all, a business being dispatched rather than a consumer being marketed to — and say on the screen when a send was deferred. Smallest high-value row in the arc. Review finding 3 | PRD MAINT-03/NOTIF-05; R-020/R-025/R-029; review §3 | S | — | Arc 5 |
| 195 | ✅ R-208 | **WRONG — nothing creates a move-in condition report, nothing notices its absence, and the job that claims to escalate a missing one cannot fire.** A `MOVE_IN` inspection is created in exactly one place, a staff member pressing the button (`apps/web/lib/inspections/actions.ts:85,130`); lease activation creates none, and `lease.deposit_cleared` raises a Task about access codes that says nothing about a walk (`apps/web/lib/leases/deposit-clearing-job.ts:103-111`). The apparent safety net is not one: `inspection.move_in_overdue` selects `{ type: 'MOVE_IN', selfGuided: true, performedAt: null }` (`apps/web/lib/inspections/auto-finalize-job.ts:122-130`) — **it needs the row to exist before it can complain nobody walked it**. The asymmetry is provable in two filenames: `pre-move-out-scheduling-job.ts:78` creates its inspection automatically and there is no move-in equivalent. What it costs is the entire deposit case: `move-out-copy.ts:39` builds R-070/R-151's side-by-side from `{ leaseId, type: 'MOVE_IN' }`, so with none the comparison screen has nothing on the left and every deduction trips `isUnsupportedDeduction` (`packages/core/ledger/disposition.ts:95-105`). **Needs counsel** on the penalty exposure, not on the gap. Fix: raise the MOVE_IN inspection on lease activation (blank, `selfGuided` per the tenant's arrangement) so the existing overdue job has something to watch, and loudly warn on the access-code release while no move-in report exists. **Warn, never block** — D-187's posture; a family must not be stopped moving in on a Saturday because nobody pressed a button. Review finding 4 | PRD INSP-01/INSP-02; R-068/R-069/R-074; D-178/D-187; review §4 | M | — | Arc 5 |
| 196 | ✅ R-209 | **WRONG — the deposit applied to arrears never reaches the ledger, so the tenancy still shows the whole balance after the letter says it is settled.** `computeDisposition` sets `appliedCents = min(heldCents, deductions + outstandingLedgerCents)` (`packages/core/ledger/disposition.ts:52-56`) and `finalizeDisposition` writes that number onto the `Deposit` row and into the letter (`apps/web/lib/deposits/actions.ts:284-292`) — and writes **no ledger entry at all**; a grep for a credit posting on the disposition path returns nothing. Tenant moves out owing $1,500 with $1,500 held: the letter correctly says the deposit was applied and nothing refunded, the `Deposit` liability is released (`disposition.ts:208-232`), and the tenancy's `balanceCents` is **still $1,500, for ever**. Four readers then disagree with the letter the tenant is holding — the lease page's balance, `statementForPeriod` (including R-083's attorney packet), the operating report's income side, and anything handed to a collections agency. Two books, one event, and the one saying $1,500 is the one somebody acts on; demanding money already taken out of the deposit is the fact pattern that turns a clean move-out into a counterclaim. D-11 governs the fix and does not block it: the application is money that satisfied a receivable, so it goes to Stripe as a credit (the `waiveCharge` path at `apps/web/lib/ledger/waivers.ts:118-140` is the shape) and comes back as a projected entry. **Do not backfill** — report the affected deposits, fix the writer. Review finding 5 | PRD PAY-07/PAY-11/INSP-05; D-11; R-071/R-170; review §5 | M | D-227 | Arc 5 |
| 197 | ✅ R-210 | **WRONG — a `Notice` records service the product never proved, on the one channel the tenant may not be able to reach.** Five call sites stamp `serviceMethod: 'PORTAL', servedAt: now` plus a `NoticeDelivery` row plus a `notice.served` audit row, unconditionally, inside the transaction, before anything is sent: work-order scheduling (`apps/web/lib/workorders/scheduling.ts:186-243`), inspection scheduling (`inspections/scheduling.ts:188,213`), showings (`showings/actions.ts:126,142`), lease actions (`leases/actions.ts:857,881`) and chargebacks (`workorders/chargeback-actions.ts:213`). The comment in the first defends it — *"an entry notice is delivered to the portal by the product itself, so there is no operator choice to check"* — which is the exact reasoning **D-179 overturned for notifications**: a portal is not an address unless the person can get into it. The fix was scoped to `NotificationDelivery` and never reached the `Notice` table. Not theoretical: `deliverAuthLink` sends on `account_access`, EMAIL-only by construction (`apps/web/lib/auth/delivery.ts:33-36,92-96`), so a tenant with a phone and no email **cannot sign in at all** (R-216) and the product records a 24-hour entry notice as served to them through that portal. There is no `lastSignedInAt` on `Tenant`, so the claim is not even falsifiable — and an unlawful-entry or lockout claim is argued entirely off that timestamp. Same class, different channel: the FCRA adverse-action notice writes `serviceMethod: 'EMAIL', servedAt: now` inside the transaction (`apps/web/lib/screening/staff-actions.ts:181-186`) while the real send is a `try/catch` afterwards whose failure branch is one `console.error` (`:231-233`). Fix: record what the engine actually did. PORTAL service requires a working sign-in path and prefers `markNoticeRead`'s read receipt as the honest evidence; EMAIL service is written from the delivery outcome, not from optimism. Extends D-179. Review finding 6 | PRD COMM-02/LEASE-11/PROP-04; D-179; R-027/R-051/R-061/R-157; review §6 | M | R-216 | Arc 5 |
| 198 | ✅ R-211 | **WRONG — a suppressed notification is reported to the operator as sent, and 36 of 39 callers never look.** `notify()` returns `ChannelOutcome[]` carrying `status?: 'QUEUED' \| 'SUPPRESSED' \| 'DEFERRED'` and a `reason` (`apps/web/lib/notifications/send.ts:87-100`), and its own header says a suppressed row is *"most of the value of the table."* Thirty-nine modules call it; **three read the status** (`plan-actions.ts:261`, `messages.ts:152`, `announcement-actions.ts:186`). The sharpest instance is the rent chase: `sendReminders` does `sentTo.push(party.id)` immediately after `notify()` with no test of the outcome (`apps/web/lib/payments/reminders.ts:196-222`), writes `message.bulk_sent` with a `skipped` list that only ever holds template failures (`:236-253`), and tells the operator *"Reminder sent to 3 people on 2 tenancies"* (`:262-274`) — counting a guarantor with no TCPA consent (correctly suppressed per D-190/D-211), a tenant who texted STOP, a recipient with neither email nor phone, and anyone inside quiet hours. The append-only `Notification` table says SUPPRESSED and the audit row a PM or an attorney reads says sent: **two contradictory records of one event, and the wrong one is on the screen.** Same lie on `workorders/scheduling.ts:315` (*"Scheduled, and the tenant has been told"*) and on every entry-notice and showing path. Fix at the shared helper, never at 39 call sites: count `QUEUED`/`DEFERRED` as sent and everything else as skipped-with-a-reason. `plan-actions.ts:278`'s sentence — *"Not sent to X — no email or phone we may use; give them a copy yourself"* — is the model and already exists. Closes R-196's leftover. Review finding 7 | PRD NOTIF-01/NOTIF-05/COMM-04; R-016/R-173/R-196; D-190/D-211; review §7 | S–M | — | Arc 5 |
| 199 | ✅ R-212 | **WRONG — the retaliation guard is off the renewal path, which is how nearly every rent increase actually reaches a tenant.** `retaliationCheckFor` has exactly two callers, both in `apps/web/lib/leases/actions.ts`: a direct rent raise on the lease edit form (`:355-362`) and a landlord-given notice to vacate (`:775-782`). `offerRenewal` (`apps/web/lib/leases/renewal-actions.ts:101-145`) checks the statutory increase cap and the notice period and **never asks the retaliation question**; neither does `openEvictionCase` (`apps/web/lib/evictions/actions.ts:78`, which demands a written reason and never checks the window) nor serving a notice through `/notices`, R-194's cure notice included. So the guard is armed on the path used by hand a few times a year and disarmed on the path that produces almost every rent increase in a portfolio — the 120/90-day renewal offer R-065 built to make renewals routine. Concretely: no-hot-water ticket on 3 April with `habitabilityFlag: true`, renewal offer with a $125 increase on 20 April, product raises nothing; the direct-edit form would have refused without a written reason, **and that written reason is the entire defence**. Fix: call the check from `offerRenewal` (increase only), `openEvictionCase`, and cure-notice generation — the ack machinery and the audit action already exist. Second half, same row: the only signal the check reads is `Ticket.habitabilityFlag` (`apps/web/lib/leases/retaliation-check.ts:36-40`), so an `AccommodationRequest`, a code-enforcement complaint or a written repair demand arriving as an ordinary ticket opens no window at all. Review finding 8 | PRD RISK-06; R-055/R-065; review §8 | S | — | Arc 5 |
| 200 | ✅ R-213 | **WRONG — serving a cure notice stops nothing, so the demand is stale by the next morning.** R-156's serve-and-hold places only the three payment switches — `blockOnline`, `blockPartial`, `certifiedFundsOnly` (`apps/web/lib/notices/actions.ts:391-401`). None of the seven `Hold` types carrying `halt_late_fees` (`packages/core/holds/index.ts:100-150`) is placed by serving a notice or by opening an eviction case; a grep for a writer of `halt_late_fees` outside the late-fee job's own reader returns nothing. So the nightly `ledger.late_fees` job keeps running against a tenancy under a served pay-or-quit: under `DAILY` or `FLAT_PLUS_DAILY` the fee grows every night (`packages/core/ledger/late-fee.ts:172-181`) while the notice froze `demandedCents` at generation (D-209). By day three of a three-day window the two screens disagree — `cureVerdict` marks the tenant **cured** on `keptCents >= demandedCents` (`packages/core/evictions/demand.ts:197`) while `/leases/[id]` still shows a balance. In a state where a pay-or-quit must state the sum with precision a post-service charge is an argument the tenant's side gets for free, and in a state where accepting payment waives the notice the operator has been paid an amount that does not match the demand. **Needs counsel** on whether post-service accrual defeats the notice; the product fix does not wait on it — a `notice_served` hold type carrying `halt_late_fees`, offered on the same serve form that already offers the payment switches, and the cure clock showing demanded-versus-ledger side by side so the divergence is visible rather than inferred. Review finding 9 | PRD PAY-12/PAY-14; D-209; R-047/R-156/R-194; review §9 | S–M | — | Arc 5 |
| 201 | ✅ R-214 | **WRONG — a vendor who has never produced a certificate of insurance reads exactly like one whose COI is current, and nothing watches expiry.** `packages/core/vendors/dispatch.ts:131-132` puts the two lines side by side and only one is right: `w9Missing: !v.w9OnFile` against `coiExpired: v.coiExpiresOn != null && v.coiExpiresOn < now`. A vendor with `coiExpiresOn: null` — nobody ever asked, or they never sent one — comes back `coiExpired: false`, so the assign dropdown (`apps/web/components/workorders/assign-form.tsx:55`) and `/vendors` (`app/(admin)/vendors/page.tsx:33`) render them identically to a vendor with current cover. The W-9 case on the line above gets it right, which is what makes this a defect rather than a design choice. And nothing watches the date: there are `renter_insurance_expiring` and `renter_insurance_lapsed` jobs for the *tenant's* policy (R-067) and no COI equivalent anywhere in the job list, so a certificate that lapsed in February is found in November by somebody opening the vendor record. An uninsured roofer who falls, or who burns a house down, lands on the owner's policy and then on the owner — with the product's own record showing them clean, which is the worst posture available and the strongest single argument against the LLC separation the multi-entity design exists to protect. Fix: treat null as missing and say so in the label; a COI-expiry job on the same machinery as the renter's-insurance one; warn-and-log at dispatch rather than block, matching D-187. Review finding 10 | PRD MAINT-11; RISK; D-187; R-067/R-079; review §10 | S | — | Arc 5 |
| 202 | ✅ R-215 | **Money owed by a tenant who has moved out leaves the product entirely.** Three facts compose into one hole. `rentRoll` selects `status: { in: ['ACTIVE', 'MONTH_TO_MONTH'] }` (`apps/web/lib/payments/rent-roll.ts:110`), as do the dashboard's delinquency tile and `reports/queries.ts:179,213`, and the `payments.chase` job runs off the same function. `computeDisposition` produces `additionalOwedCents` — what the tenant still owes after the whole deposit is applied — and its own comment calls collecting it *"a separate, deliberately out-of-scope concern"* (`packages/core/ledger/disposition.ts:26-29`); it renders on exactly one screen (`app/(admin)/leases/[id]/deposit/page.tsx:221-227`) and in the letter's *"We will contact you separately about this balance"* (`disposition.ts:164-166`). There is no write-off, no bad-debt, no collections-placement and no former-tenant surface anywhere in the schema. So on the day the tenancy ends, the largest single receivable most small portfolios ever book — unpaid final rent plus damage beyond the deposit, routinely $2,000–$6,000 per bad exit — disappears from every list an operator reads, from the aging, from "worst first", and from the chase, surviving only as a sentence in a letter promising to be in touch. It is also the number that decides whether to place with an agency, sue in small claims, or write it off — a decision nobody can make from a screen that does not exist. Build in value order: keep ENDED leases with a positive balance on their own aged view (the arithmetic is already there); record `additionalOwedCents` as a real receivable on that view rather than only in prose; add a write-off that is a dated, reasoned, audited event and never a deletion. **Agency placement and credit reporting stay out of scope** — R-097b is vendor-gated. Review finding 11 | PRD PAY-06/PAY-11/RPT-02; review §11 | M | R-206 | Arc 5 |
| 203 | ✅ R-216 | **A tenant or guarantor with a phone and no email can never sign in, and the product keeps recording deliveries to them.** `deliverAuthLink` is the only path any sign-in link takes and it sends on `account_access`, declared EMAIL-only with the reason written next to it (`apps/web/lib/auth/delivery.ts:33-36`); the recipient it hands `notify()` carries `email` and nothing else (`:92-96`), and `tenant-magic-link` is the only tenant provider wired in `auth.ts`. So a tenant with a phone and no email address has no route into the portal at all — not a hard route, none — and the same is true of a guarantor, which R-196 recorded as its own leftover after building everything else about reaching them. **This is the persona the product keeps saying it is for**: R-021's SMS-to-ticket, R-177's SMS troubleshooting door, R-181's SMS acknowledgement and D-190's consent work all exist because some long-term tenants will never log in, and then the one thing they might need the portal for — their balance, a payment, their lease papers, a notice served to them there — is closed. R-210 is the compounding half. Fix: allow the magic link to go by SMS when that is the only address (the template and the Twilio adapter both exist, and `account_access` is already LOCKED so no preference can suppress it), or a staff-issued printed one-time code for a tenant who has neither. Whichever way it goes, `reachableElectronically`'s predicate belongs in front of every PORTAL service claim. Closes R-173's and R-196's leftovers. Review finding 12 | PRD ROLE-05/NOTIF-05; PRD §6.4's Gene test; D-179; R-003/R-139/R-173/R-196; review §12 | M | — | Arc 5 |
| 204 | ✅ R-217 | **Nothing measures a habitability complaint against a repair deadline.** `detectHabitabilityLanguage` stamps `Ticket.habitabilityFlag` at every intake door — portal, SMS, email, phone-logged (`apps/web/lib/maintenance/actions.ts:206`, `comms/sms-intake.ts:169`, `comms/email-intake.ts:138`) — and downstream it does three things: raises the suggested priority, titles the triage Task, and opens the retaliation window. **It starts no clock.** `JurisdictionRule` carries `entryNoticeHours`, `payOrQuitDays`, `leaseViolationCureDays`, `depositDispositionDays`, `abandonmentPresumedAfterDays`, `retaliationWindowDays` and a dozen more, and no repair-deadline field of any kind; nothing anywhere asks how long a habitability-flagged ticket has been open. That is the one statutory clock in this product that runs **against** the owner rather than for them, and the only major one with no machinery — in many states a written tenant notice plus a failure to repair within a defined period unlocks repair-and-deduct, rent withholding, termination and statutory damages, and this product is already holding the written notice, timestamped, in an append-only table. The ordinary version is a no-heat ticket opened on a Friday and sitting unassigned through a long weekend; the expensive version is a mould or sewage claim where the elapsed time is the whole case. **Needs counsel** on the deadline and on what counts as written notice — which is exactly why it is a `JurisdictionRule` field under D-4 and not a constant. Fix: `habitabilityRepairDays` (plus whatever acknowledgement window counsel names) on the rule; the ticket carries a derived due date; the existing window-watching job pattern plus R-191's cool-off raises the Task at halfway and at overdue; `computeCoverage` names it a `productLimit` for any state that has not answered, which is D-195's shape. Review finding 13 | PRD MAINT-01/RISK-06; PRD §6.7; D-4/D-195; R-191; review §13 | M | — | Arc 5 |
| 205 | ✅ R-218 | **A deposit-dispute packet, because the deposit case is the one a small operator actually ends up in court over.** R-083 built the one-click attorney packet for evictions and did it properly — case summary, statement of account, every notice with its proof of service, the executed lease, the photographs, every excluded exhibit named on the index and on the audit row (`apps/web/lib/evictions/packet.ts:24-52`, D-50). Nothing equivalent exists for a deposit; a grep for `packet` across `apps/web/lib` returns the eviction one, the tax one, the property handoff file and nothing on the deposit path. An owner-operator at this scale may file one eviction in three years and will face a deposit claim most years, because it is the cheapest case a former tenant can bring and the one where the burden sits squarely on the landlord. What that claim needs is one file: the move-in condition report with its item photographs and the tenant's signature (R-208 is what makes it exist at all), the move-out report beside it, R-151's per-item comparison, each deduction with the work order or invoice or inspection item it is linked to **and the depreciation guidance that was applied** (`packages/core/ledger/disposition.ts:85-92` — the part that wins and the part a hand-assembled packet reliably misses), the disposition letter, the proof of service to the forwarding address, R-170's refund payment record, and the statutory dates. Every one of those rows exists and none can be handed over as a document. Machinery is `renderBlocksPdf` + `appendPdfs` + `packetBlocks`, the same three functions R-083 used, and the same D-50 rule about naming what could not be included. Review finding 14 | PRD INSP-03/INSP-05/PAY-11; D-50; R-070/R-071/R-083/R-151/R-170; review §14 | M | R-208 | Arc 5 |
| 206 | ✅ R-219 | **Nothing starts the marketing clock when a notice to vacate lands.** R-178 made the turn a sequenced project and it is good — seven stages (`packages/core/turnover/stages.ts:13-21`), real work orders, a stall sweep, a cost roll-up (`apps/web/lib/turnover/queries.ts:149`) — and every one of those stages begins **after** the unit is empty. `createListing` has exactly one caller, the staff form (`apps/web/lib/listings/actions.ts:74-107`); nothing in `turnover/start.ts`, in the notice-to-vacate flow, or in the renewal non-renewal path creates a listing, raises a listing Task, or mentions one. Days vacant is the most expensive number in this business and the thirty days of notice are the only free days an operator ever gets — the window to photograph, price, publish and book showings against an occupied unit, and R-064 already handles the occupied-unit showing and its entry notice, so the capability is built and simply never prompted. At $1,500/month a day is $49; a week's slippage on six turns a year is **$2,058**, and two weeks across a twenty-door portfolio at 40% annual turnover is roughly **$8,200**. Fix needs no new entity: when a notice to vacate is accepted or a renewal lapses into non-renewal, raise a `listing.prepare` Task dated on the notice day, pre-fill the listing from the unit and the outgoing rent, and put days-to-list beside days-to-fill on `/reports/leasing` so the cost of not doing it is visible. **Publishing stays a deliberate act; only the prompt is automatic.** Review finding 15 | PRD LEASE-12/LEASE-01/RPT-01; D-9/D-184; R-056/R-064/R-167/R-178; review §15 | S–M | — | Arc 5 |
| 207 | ✅ R-220 | **The Arc 5 demo walk (D-28).** The milestone rule, and the review says so itself in the section on what it structurally could not see: it read code and schema, so *"the screen says something quietly wrong"* is not reachable from it and **the next milestone still owes its D-28 walk**. The precedent is unbroken — R-105 found seven defects across 88 routes that all returned 200, R-184 three across 250, R-204 eight across 236 staff routes plus five portal sessions, and in each case the test suite saw none of them; R-204's packet defect (D-221) was invisible to every screen as well. Walk it in a browser at desktop **and** 412px phone width, both Playwright projects, and walk the five public token surfaces R-204 left unwalked — bid, apply, prescreen, showing, pay — since `db:seed:demo-access` prints only the vendor job link. Two standing seed gaps to check while there: the demo seed creates no payment plan, no deposit batch, records no SCRA termination and books no property expense, so several shipped screens have never been seen with data in them. Crawl scripts are deliberately not committed (R-204's reasoning); two gotchas if you write the third — a script outside the repo root cannot resolve `@playwright/test`, and `/workorders/[id]/timeline` is a download, so `page.goto` throws *"Download is starting"* rather than returning a response. Review §"What this review structurally could not see" | PRD §8; D-28/D-153/D-154/D-196/D-197; R-105/R-184/R-204 | M | R-219 | Arc 5 |
| 208 | ✅ R-221 | **Accrual-basis income is missing every month of subscription rent, on the tax export and the operating report.** `taxExportFacts` picks the income table off the basis (`apps/web/lib/tax/queries.ts:106-150`): cash reads `LedgerEntry` rows carrying a payment, accrual reads the `Charge` table. D-11/D-40 settled that the subscription's rent line mints **no `Charge` row**, so accrual income is only late fees, prorations and the other charge-minting extras. Measured on the demo database at R-220: 14 `LedgerEntry` rows of type `CHARGE` with no `chargeId` carry **$26,550** of rent that accrual cannot see, against 5 charge-linked rows worth $4,770.75 that it can. On the screen that is an operating report reading `$851.61` income for a house billing $2,200 a month, economic occupancy of **2%**, and a Schedule E line that understates rent income — Arc 5's own theme (R-205/R-206: three readers written against `Charge` as if it were the debt list) in a fourth reader nobody has checked. The candidate source is the ledger's own `CHARGE` rows, which is what an invoice posting writes whether or not a `Charge` row exists — **the item's real work is arguing that the charge-linked ones are not then counted twice**, and deciding what a waived charge means on that side (the current accrual read excludes `waivedAt`, the ledger records a reversing entry instead). Reconciliation identity in `packages/core/tax` has a test asserting the totals; extend it rather than write a second one. Found by R-220's demo walk, not by any test. **Built (D-239): the accrual ledger read is the exact COMPLEMENT of the cash one, which is what makes double-counting impossible rather than unlikely** — cash takes the rows carrying a payment, accrual takes `paymentId: null` AND `chargeId: null`, and `webhook.ts` writes one LINKED entry per `Charge` row it raised plus one UNLINKED remainder for subscription rent, so a linked ledger row is a projection OF a row the `Charge` table already supplies. Proved on the demo database: 91 charges, 90 of which have a linked ledger row, against 238 unlinked rent rows — no overlap by construction. `REVERSAL` joins `CHARGE` because it is the ledger's spelling of a waiver (append-only, so a void writes a negative row instead of amending). **The real defect was not the missing rows but the SIGN**: `buildTaxExport` negated every ledger row, correct for a cash receipt (negative, reduces the balance) and exactly backwards for an accrual charge (positive, raises the obligation), so the first version of this fix reported NEGATIVE rent. The flip is now conditioned on the basis. Bluebonnet Lane House 2026 accrual income $851.61 → $7,451.61. | D-11/D-40; D-71/D-72; R-078/R-081a; R-205/R-206; R-220; D-239 | M | — | Arc 5 |

## Milestone 16: Arc 6 — two automated processes that are each correct alone contradict each other on one tenancy

Sourced from the 2026-09-17 operator review (`docs/reviews/2026-09-17-operator-review.md`), commissioned by D-240 after Arc 5 closed at R-221. Ordering follows the review: the **ten** WRONG findings first, then the three recorded leftovers that are now load-bearing. Four rows are **Needs counsel** and say so. The theme, in the review's words: Arc 5's defects were reports reading the wrong numbers; **Arc 6's are two automated paths that each pass their own tests and contradict each other on the same tenancy, with no test that runs both** — the 03:00 make-ready job and the 04:00 month-to-month rollover, the portal's emergency path and every other intake door, the renewal's rent checks and the lease edit form's. Six of the thirteen are leftovers an Arc 5 `PROGRESS.md` entry recorded as owned by nobody. Each row cites the review section that holds the file-and-line evidence; read that section before starting.

| # | ID | Item | PRD/Feature | Size | Depends on | Phase |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 209 | ✅ R-222 | **WRONG — the morning a lease lapses to month-to-month, the 03:00 job marks the occupied house vacant.** `unit.auto_make_ready` (`apps/web/lib/units/auto-make-ready.ts:37`, `:58-64`) selects `ACTIVE`/`MONTH_TO_MONTH` leases with `endsOn < today` on an `OCCUPIED` unit and checks only for a successor lease; `lease.mtm_rollover` (`renewal-rollover-job.ts:28`, `:46`) selects the same lease an hour later and keeps it. Between them the unit goes `MAKE_READY`, `Lease.moveOutAt` is stamped with a move-out that never happened, `retireUnitAccessCodes` ends the family's codes, R-178's turn opens with its re-key work order, and staff are emailed — while Stripe keeps billing. A tenant under notice who holds over past `endsOn` gets the same. Spot-checked against source before this row was written (D-240). **Fix:** only a recorded move-out or an ended status makes a unit ready — exclude any lease the rollover will keep and any lease under notice with no `moveOutAt`; one test drives both jobs against one lease on one business date. **Do not backfill `moveOutAt`**; whether live-tenancy units are set back to `OCCUPIED` is a separate decision row (review, "Do not build"). **Needs counsel** on the self-help-eviction reading of the record. **Shipped (D-241):** the job acts only on a lease with a recorded `moveOutAt`; the `endsOn` stamp is deleted. Review finding 1 | PRD PROP-02/LEASE-09/LEASE-12; R-009/R-065/R-176/R-178; D-178/D-182/D-184 | M | — | Arc 6 |
| 210 | ✅ R-223 | **WRONG — an emergency that arrives by text, email or phone can never page anybody, and staff cannot mark one.** `priority: 'EMERGENCY'` has one writer, the portal's `submitEmergency` (`maintenance/actions.ts:417`). `suggestTicketPriority` returns only `'URGENT' \| 'ROUTINE'` (`packages/core/maintenance/priority.ts:35-40`) and staff triage offers only `TRIAGE_PRIORITY_OPTIONS = ['URGENT', 'ROUTINE']` (`actions.ts:683`) — so the initial page, R-029's 15-minute escalation and R-207's after-hours dispatch never fire for three of four intake doors, and a comment in `triage-consumer.ts` says otherwise. Spot-checked (D-240). **Fix:** `EMERGENCY` in staff triage, running the same paging-and-dispatch path as the portal and auditing who escalated; habitability language on SMS/email/phone intake *suggests* an emergency to the on-call person with the tenant's words attached — a human confirms. **Not** auto-paging on any message containing "water". Review finding 2 | PRD MAINT-01/MAINT-03/NOTIF-05; R-020/R-021/R-029/R-097f/R-207; D-18 | S–M | — | Arc 6 |
| 211 | ✅ R-224 | **WRONG (live mode) — a tenant cannot cure a pay-or-quit at the counter.** The live Stripe lookup fetches one open invoice, so with partial payments blocked a cheque for the full arrears is refused as more than invoiced and a one-month cheque as partial. The simulator returns one invoice and agrees with us by construction (D-27), so no test or demo can reach it. R-209 and R-215 recorded the same root on the deposit and damages paths. **Fix:** list open invoices by customer, apply the offline payment oldest-first in `ledger/allocate.ts`'s order, and make the simulator return one invoice per billed period so the multi-invoice branch is testable. **Needs counsel** on refusing a tendered cure. Review finding 3 | PRD PAY-05/PAY-12/PAY-14/INSP-05; R-038/R-038a/R-155/R-156/R-209/R-215; D-26/D-31/D-142 | M | — | Arc 6 |
| 212 | ✅ R-225 | **WRONG — a rent increase typed on the lease edit form reaches Stripe with no notice period, no cap check and no notice document.** Only the renewal path reads the jurisdiction's notice and cap rules, and the edit form is the only way to raise rent on a month-to-month tenancy. **Fix:** the edit form's raise carries an effective date, runs `renewalCheck`'s cap and notice logic with an override reason as `offerRenewal` does, generates a rent-increase `Notice` through R-051, and cuts over to Stripe only on the effective date (R-065's deferred shape). **Needs counsel** on each state's period. Review finding 4 | PRD LEASE-09 / PRD §6.7; R-033/R-055/R-065/R-200; D-4/D-12/D-56 | S–M | — | Arc 6 |
| 213 | ✅ R-226 | **WRONG — a tenant who renewed has no move-in side to their deposit case, and the code says they do.** The move-out comparison and R-218's dispute packet look for the move-in report only under the current lease; nothing follows `renewedFromLeaseId` back. **Fix:** one `baselineMoveInFor(leaseId)` walking to the tenancy's first lease, read by `itemsFromMoveIn`, the packet, `deposit-clearing-job.ts`'s warning and `Lease.moveInAt`'s readers; correct the comment in `move-in-consumer.ts`. **Not** a new inspection demanded of a three-year tenant. Review finding 5 | PRD INSP-01/INSP-03/INSP-05/PAY-11; R-065/R-070/R-151/R-208/R-218; D-54/D-60/D-236 | M | — | Arc 6 |
| 214 | ✅ R-227 | **WRONG — serving a cure notice switches late fees off for the rest of the tenancy.** The `NOTICE_SERVED` hold is never lifted. **Fix:** lift it automatically when R-194's cure verdict is reached or the case closes, recorded `liftedBySystem` with the reason as the plan job does, and show the hold's age on `/leases/[id]`. Report the fees a forgotten hold suppressed; do not backfill them. Review finding 6 | PRD PAY-04/PAY-14; R-084/R-213; D-34/D-78/D-231 | S | — | Arc 6 |
| 215 | ✅ R-228 | **WRONG — an entry is judged as though its notice were served this instant, and preventive work enters occupied houses with no notice at all.** An unserved notice clears the entry check; a work order with no ticket never resolves a tenancy. **Fix:** resolve the tenancy from the unit's in-force lease rather than the ticket; judge against the notice's real `servedAt`, routing an unserved one through the existing override with its reason; hold preventive work orders on occupied units as unscheduled until a notice is served. **Needs counsel** on damages for entries already made. Review finding 7 | PRD MAINT-05/COMM-02/RISK-06; R-027/R-080/R-157/R-210/R-211; D-47/D-228 | M | — | Arc 6 |
| 216 | ✅ R-229 | **WRONG — the chase ladder speaks three times in a tenancy's first arrears episode and never again.** **Fix:** re-arm per unpaid period, keyed on the newest unpaid period's days past grace; add a standing URGENT "decide: plan, notice or write-off" Task at grace + 30, repeated weekly under R-191's cool-off while the balance stands. `halt_dunning` still suppresses all of it. **Not** a settings screen for `CHASE_LADDER_DAYS` — five reviews have now declined it. | 217 | ✅ R-230 | **WRONG — a vacant unit with no asking rent counts as $0 of vacancy loss**, so economic occupancy reads better than it is. **Fix:** price it from the last lease's rent when there is one, show "+N unpriced" beside the total, and report economic occupancy as null with a reason when any vacant unit in the window is unpriced — the posture `economicOccupancy` already takes for a zero denominator. ## Milestone 17: Go-live hardening

The numbered backlog closed at R-236 with all 16 prior milestones ✅ (owner decision, 2026-09-21, D-253). This milestone is not features — it is what stands between "every planned item is built" and "safe to put a real owner's rent and a real tenant's data through it." Sourced from three places: the quiet-hours e2e fragility R-236 found and did not fix; the carried-defects list that has accumulated, unowned, since Arc 4 (each one recorded in the `NEXT.md` history and its origin item's `PROGRESS.md`); and the three unresolved owner questions at the bottom of this file ("Flagged gaps & conflicts", items 4-6). Only the first is scoped into a buildable row today — the rest need a scoping pass before they are rows, not before they are known.

The test file's fixture and the orphan share the same `effectiveFrom` (2020-01-01) and both have `jurisdiction: null`, so the `@@unique([state, jurisdiction, version])` constraint never fires (Postgres treats null as distinct) and `rulesFor`'s `findMany` has no `ORDER BY` — `selectApplicableRule`'s tie-break needs a *strictly later* `effectiveFrom` to prefer one row over another, so with two identical dates the winner is whichever row Postgres's query planner returns first, which is not stable across runs. Exactly the "wrong data, no thrown error" signature R-238 found. **Fix:** `case-stall-job.test.ts`'s `STATE` constant, previously the fixed literal `'QZ'`, is now `` `Q${randomUUID().slice(0, 8)}` `` — genuinely unique per run, the same pattern `e2e/fixtures.ts`'s `uniqueStateCode()` already uses for this exact bug class ("not across workers, and not against the rows a crashed run left behind"). Deleted the one orphaned row found in `rental_test`. **Not fixed:** the underlying possibility of two `JurisdictionRule` rows tying on `(state, jurisdiction, effectiveFrom)` in production — out of scope; this row only removes the fixed-literal fixture that let a dead run's debris collide with a live one. | PRD RISK-06/MAINT-01; R-217/R-238 (found, not fixed) | S | — | Go-live |

Same bug shape as R-239 (a tie an `ORDER BY` cannot resolve on its own), different column. **Fix:** `auditTrailFor` now sorts `[{ occurredAt: 'asc' }, { id: 'asc' }]` — `id` is a cuid, monotonic per process, so it breaks the tie in true creation order, matching the array-orderBy tiebreak pattern already used throughout `apps/web/lib/**/queries.ts`. **Not fixed:** the underlying tie itself (transaction-stable timestamps) is correct Postgres behavior, not a bug — only the missing tiebreaker was. No other `auditTrailFor` caller exists outside a comment reference in `packages/core/tasks/registry.ts`, so widening the sort is risk-free. | PRD ROLE-03; R-236 (carried, unowned) | S | — | Go-live |

## Rationale: ordering decisions & trade-offs

1. **Foundation before any feature (R-001–R-013).** Every module reads and writes the shared schema and assumes property scoping, RBAC, audit logging, an event bus, and jurisdiction rules. Building these once, first, prevents each module re-inventing them.
2. **The jurisdiction-rules engine is foundation, not a compliance feature (R-010).** It is deliberately the largest Milestone 1 item. Every later module reads statutory numbers from it; if it arrives late, dozens of hardcoded grace periods and deadlines have to be hunted down, and the ones missed are silent. Its resolver signature — `rulesFor(property, asOf)` — is what makes rule changes prospective rather than retroactive.
3. **The task queue before its first consumer (R-011).** The storage build had to consolidate seven separate queues after the fact. This is that lesson spent once, in advance, for the cost of building R-011 before anything needs it.
4. **Maintenance is Golden Path 1, ahead of money.** Two reasons. It forces the five hardest *shared* subsystems into existence early — notification engine, comms threading, photo evidence, magic-link identity, task queue — which the money loop then inherits rather than builds. And it is the differentiated 30% that justifies a custom build at all: rent collection is available off the shelf; a vendor who fixes things by text without an account is not. Building it first also means the riskiest adoption assumption (D-6, vendors will use magic links) gets tested in week 6 rather than month 5.
5. **Stripe Billing owns collection; core owns every number a statute could change (R-034, R-035; D-11/D-12).** Delegating invoicing, retries and ACH-return handling removes a genuine pile of scheduler code, and it is the more common architecture in industry — worth learning deliberately, which is why this repo diverges from the storage platform rather than repeating it. The discipline that makes it safe is the split: Stripe executes, core decides. A late fee Stripe generated is a late fee that ignores the state cap, and a fee that ignores the state cap is one a tenant's lawyer gets to talk about. The projection is built from webhooks and **reconciled on a schedule with drift alarmed** — the failure mode of an event-sourced read model is silent divergence, and money that silently diverges is the worst bug this system can have.
6. **Notices and evidence get their own milestone (R-051–R-055).** Proof-of-delivery, immutable transcripts and the retaliation guard are uniform requirements across leasing, billing and maintenance. Building them as a milestone rather than per-module is why the eviction packet (R-083) and the deposit disposition (R-071) are later assembly jobs instead of new plumbing.
7. **Leasing after the operating core.** Leasing events happen 10–20 times a year; maintenance happens weekly and rent monthly. Leasing also carries all three external integrations, so it benefits from landing on a stable base — and its simulated adapters (D-7) mean an unavailable screening partner never blocks the build.
8. **Screening's compliance machinery is built even though the provider is simulated (R-060, R-061).** Criteria versioning, order-of-application processing, decision audit and adverse-action generation are the parts with legal consequence, and they are fully testable against a simulator. Swapping in a real bureau later is a driver, not a redesign.
9. **Risk workflows are Phase 2, deliberately, with two exceptions.** Habitability auto-elevation (R-023) and the retaliation guard (R-055) ship in MVP because both are cheap once the comms record exists and both are existential if absent. The rest — abandonment, VAWA, SCRA, ESA, hoarding — are real but rarer, and each is a case file over machinery MVP already built.
10. **Real vendor drivers last (R-093).** Following the storage build's precedent: partner-gated integrations are the wrong thing to be blocked on in a learning project, and a simulator with fault injection is a better test fixture than a sandbox you don't control.

## Flagged gaps & conflicts

Unlike the storage backlog at the same stage, this list is short — there is one master PRD rather than six, so most cross-document conflicts don't exist. What remains:

1. ~~**Autopay and partial payments are mutually exclusive under D-11 (OQ-11) — this blocks Milestone 3.**~~ **RESOLVED 2026-08-10 (D-29).** The owner took option (a): collection method is a per-payer column on `LeasePayer`, autopay tenants on `charge_automatically` and partial-paying tenants on `send_invoice`, switched as their circumstances change. Both Must stories ship in full. Milestone 3 is unblocked. **What moved from a decision to a build problem**: a mid-cycle switch has to settle or explicitly void the open invoice on the old mode before the new mode's subscription bills, or the tenant is asked for the same rent twice — R-037 owns proving that with a test.
2. ~~**Section 8 phasing (R-048).**~~ **CLOSED 2026-08-24 (D-136).** The schema half closed 2026-08-01 (D-13) — R-002 shipped the payer-agnostic ledger, so the expensive retrofit this risk warned about can no longer happen. The phasing half is now closed too, by an answer rather than by a plan: OQ-2 is **no voucher tenancies, none planned**, so R-048 is cut and there is nothing left to schedule. **The risk itself was retired by D-13, not by the cut** — that is the distinction worth keeping. If a voucher tenancy appears tomorrow the work is a feature build, not a migration, which is exactly what this entry was written to guarantee.
3. ~~**Offline tech flow vs all-vendor operation (R-028).**~~ **RESOLVED 2026-08-06 (D-17).** OQ-3 answered "all external vendors", so R-028 moved to Phase 3 and Milestone 2 shortened by an L exactly as anticipated. R-030's listed dependency on R-028 is soft — verify-and-close needs work orders reaching WORK_COMPLETE, which R-025's vendor path already does — so R-030 is not blocked.
4. ~~**Screening cannot start without written criteria (OQ-6).**~~ **The BUILD half closed 2026-08-18 (07-decisions.md's own OQ-6 row): R-060 shipped with a seeded placeholder (`reviewedBy: null`) rather than blocking, the same call D-52 made for the lease template.** What stayed open — real-world use — had no mechanism at all until R-242 (2026-09-22, this milestone): there was no admin screen to record an attorney's review or replace the placeholder numbers, only the seed script. `/screening-criteria` now exists (owner-only write, `screening.criteria.write`, mirroring `jurisdiction.write`'s "legal release gate" posture) and shows an explicit unreviewed warning on the current version. **Still genuinely open**: nobody has actually reviewed the seeded numbers yet — that is item 6 below, now, plus a real attorney's sign-off, not a code gap.
5. ~~**Spanish surface may be Must, not Phase 3 (OQ-9).**~~ **RESOLVED 2026-08-24 (D-122).** OQ-9 was answered "every tenant is English-primary, none planned" — same shape as OQ-2/OQ-4 above, cut rather than deferred (D-121's reasoning). R-096 is cut; R-049's translation-template machinery survives unused for the day a non-English-primary household appears. This item was still describing R-096 as Phase 3 pending an answer a month after the answer landed — caught during the R-242 staleness pass this milestone's own "Not yet scoped" list called for.
6. **Deposit-deadline automation is a liability if the config is wrong (R-071, R-010).** The system enforcing a countdown means the system can enforce the *wrong* countdown at scale. Legal review of each jurisdiction config before activation is a release gate, not a nice-to-have — recorded here because it is a process requirement no code review will catch. **Largely already built, not yet exercised**: `computeCoverage`/`UNREVIEWED_FIELDS` (R-162) already surfaces every unreviewed statutory field on `/jurisdiction`, and a brand-new jurisdiction's founding version already refuses to save without a citation and reviewer. What R-242 found missing was the *screening-criteria* half of the same posture (item 4 above) — `JurisdictionRule` had the admin surface, `ScreeningCriteria` had none. Neither this product's seeded TX jurisdiction rule nor its v1 screening criteria has actually been reviewed by an attorney yet; that review, not more code, is what closes this item for real.

## Security findings — saas-foundation audit (2026-09-23)

Source: `~/Projects/saas foundation/FOUNDATION_SPEC.md` §3 (read-only audit). Line numbers are as of 2026-09-23 — **re-verify before fixing**. IDs are `SEC-nn` so they cannot collide with this repo's numbering; convert to a native item when picked up. ✔ = re-read by the main session; others reported by an audit agent with file:line.

**Context:** live at rent.labintelligence.co. The project is at closure; these reopen it.

| ID | Sev | Finding | Fix | Acceptance |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| ~~SEC-01~~ ✅ | **MED** | ✔ Unguarded exports in `'use server'` files: `markNoticeRead(noticeId, leaseIds)` takes caller-supplied `leaseIds` (`apps/web/lib/notices/actions.ts:1,524`) — a forged read receipt is the legal evidence of portal service. Also `sendPrescreenInvite` (`lib/prospects/actions.ts:187`), `sendShowingInvite` (`lib/showings/actions.ts:339`), `propertyForTenant` (`lib/consent/actions.ts:38`). Whether Next exposes an action ID for each is UNVERIFIED; the repo's own rule (`lib/payments/plan-actions.ts:300-302`) says every export is public. | Move these helpers to a `server-only` module, or guard them. | Every export of every `'use server'` file rejects an anonymous call (test globs the files). |
| ~~SEC-02~~ ✅ | LOW-MED | ✔ `addLeaseTenant` checks the lease but not the tenant: `prisma.tenant.findUnique({ where: { id: tenantId } })` (`apps/web/lib/leases/actions.ts:1079-1084`); `Tenant` is global. A manager scoped to property A can attach and then read a tenant from entity B. | Resolve the tenant through the actor's scope. | Out-of-scope tenant id → not-found. |
| ~~SEC-03~~ ✅ | LOW | ✔ Write actions gate records against a scope built from **`property.read`** (`lib/scope/current-scope.ts:40`): `lib/payments/deposit-actions.ts:46-81`, `lib/property-expenses/actions.ts:172-179`, `lib/vendor-invoices/actions.ts:61-97`. | Build the scope from the write permission. | Staff with write on A and read on B cannot write on B. |
| ~~SEC-04~~ ✅ | LOW | ✔ The Resend webhook has no timestamp tolerance, so signed callbacks can be replayed (`packages/core/comms/webhook-signature.ts:96-136`). | Add the same window the Stripe verifier uses. | Stale timestamp → 403 (D-261). |
| ~~SEC-05~~ ✅ | LOW | ✔ Inbound-email secret accepted as `?secret=` (`app/api/email/inbound/route.ts:113`), so it lands in access logs. | Header only. | Query-string secret → 403 (D-262). |
| ~~SEC-06~~ ✅ | LOW | ✔ No Referrer-Policy header (`next.config.ts`) while bearer tokens live in URL paths (/pay, /sign, /vendor, /verify, /api/calendar). | Add to `headers()` or the proxy that already sets the nonce CSP. | Header asserted on a token route. |
| ~~SEC-07~~ ✅ | LOW | ✔ `/api/documents/[id]/file` sends no `Cache-Control` on private documents (`route.ts:97`); its guarantor branch skips the `deletedAt` check (~`route.ts:58`) that `tenantCanSeeDocument` has (`core/portal/access.ts:106`). | `private, no-store`; check `deletedAt`. | Deleted document → 404 for a guarantor. |
| ~~SEC-08~~ ✅ | **HIGH** | ✔ SEC-06's `Referrer-Policy: no-referrer` makes Chrome send `Origin: null` on a native form POST, so Next refuses every Server Action submitted without JavaScript ("Invalid Server Actions request"). CI run `35924753371`: `maintenance.spec.ts:158` and `vendor-link.spec.ts:376` red on both projects; production affected from SEC-06's deploy. | `same-origin`: still sends nothing cross-site, keeps Origin on same-site posts. | The two no-JS specs pass; header asserted as `same-origin`. |
| ~~R-249~~ ✅ | LOW | ✔ `assessNsfFee` audits `ledger.adjusted` with no `reason` (`apps/web/lib/ledger/nsf-fees.ts:167`); that action is on `REASON_REQUIRED`, so `recordAudit` threw into a swallowing `.catch` and no NSF fee was ever audited. Swept every `REASON_REQUIRED` call site: this was the only one. | Pass `reasonCode: 'other'` and the fee's description as `reason`, as `proration.ts` does. | An NSF fee has a `ledger.adjusted` audit row whose reason is the fee description. |
